Foreword

The New York Patent, Trademark and Copyright Law As sociation, Inc., one of the oldest regional associations in the country, was founded in 1922. The association currently has over 1,100 members in the area encompassed by the Second

Judicial Circuit (New York, Connecticut, Vermont) and the Dis trict of New Jersey. It has had a long history of supporting and enhancing intellectual property law protection and development and its administration. For example, its members were instrumental in the codification of patent laws in 1952, which is still the backbone of substantive patent law today. So, too, were its members instrumental in drafting and promoting the enactment of 35 U.S.C. §294 providing for voluntary arbitration.

This section provides for the enforcement of arbitration agreements relating to validity and infringement of patents. The legislation assures parties of the finality needed to enable them to submit disputes to the flexible, relatively inexpensive, and quick arbitration process.

The association has always been interested in arbitration; it was one of the first organizations to have an Arbitration Committee. The Arbitration Committee has been very active, and this guide is the culmination of several years of work of the committee. Its members have given generously of their time and effort in the true spirit of public service from the bar. 

The litigation of patent disputes has been the subject of criticism by the bar, the bench, and the public. Although patent disputes are usually very well-prepared and well-tried, such thoroughness is time-consuming and costly. Alternatives to conventional discovery and trial as a way to resolve disputes have received much attention in recent years. However, inertia is a powerful force in the law, as elsewhere, in resisting change.

vi 

Guide to Patent Arbitration 

The bar is familiar with the practices and procedures of litigation and, accordingly, relatively comfortable in advising clients on what can and cannot be done within such a framework. What is needed, in order to enlarge the bar's perspective about arbitration as an alternative to litigation, is a guide such as this. 

This book provides a thorough and practical analysis of the arbitration procedure. It fills a void in most lawyers' education. A valuable contribution which this book can, and I am sure will, make is to force every lawyer who advises a client to stop and think through the question is arbitration a viable alternative in this dispute? This guide should assist lawyers in using a means to resolve disputes more quickly, in a less costly manner, and in a way that will reduce the burden on the judiciary-without sacrificing justice. We all owe a great deal to the contributors who made this book possible. 

PAUL H. HELLER President, NYPTCLA October 1987

Preface 

 is intended to acquaint general practitioners, as well as those specializing in the field of intellectual property, with methods of resolving patent disputes other than through traditional court proceedings. It explains arbitration as well as other new forms of dispute resolution, such as mini-trials.

The impetus for writing this book was the passage of 35 U.S.C. §294 in 1982. That law overruled some earlier cases which held that the question of a patent's validity was so involved with the public interest that only a federal court could consider such a question. The new law specifically authorized arbitration of future and pending disputes concerning validity and infringement of patents.

The primary reason for this new law was dissatisfaction by many with traditional means for resolution of intellectual property disputes, and concern about the ever-growing cost, complexity, and disruption involved in court proceedings. With the litigation explosion, cases were also taking an inordinate amount of time to decide. Chief Justice Burger, in his address to the 

American Bar Association in 1982, urged practitioners to consider arbitration as a viable alternative to traditional court proceedings. He and others have repeated the plea many times. 

Since the passage of §294, several other intellectual property statutes have been passed which encourage the use of arbitration. The Patent Law Amendments of 1984 is one such act. This law adds a subsection (d) to 35 U.S.C. §135 permitting parties to a patent interference to "determine such contest or any aspect thereof by arbitration." Similar arbitration provisions vii

viii 

 were included in the statute granting intellectual property protection for computer chips. 

The advantages of arbitration are well known: arbitration is usually cheaper and faster than litigation; it possesses no evidentiary rules; it minimizes disruption and hostility and promotes further future business dealings among parties; it provides flexibility in scheduling; it provides confidentiality; and it provides an opportunity for the parties to select expert arbitrators of their own choice who are familiar with the particular technology involved. 

Another much-discussed informal dispute resolution technique is the mini-trial. More like a structured negotiation than an adjudication, the mini-trial usually involves a neutral person who guides the proceedings and who may play a larger role if the parties wish. Mini-trials may proceed on an ad hoc basis, but procedures have been published by, for example, the American Arbitration Association, The Center for Public Resources, and the Zurich Chamber of Commerce. A chapter on these techniques is included at the end of the guide. 

This book is arranged for quick and easy access to areas of particular interest. First, we look quickly at some general aspects of dispute resolution and patent dispute arbitration and case law in particular. Then we cover the provisions and use of §294 in detail, as well as the general federal law applicable to patent arbitration and the American Arbitration Association Patent Arbitration Rules. Next this book addresses some practical problems in arbitration, such as how to frame the issues, how to select arbitrators, and how to enforce awards. It also touches very briefly on arbitration of other types of intellectual property disputes, but these are generally outside the scope of this guide.

The book also provides an overview of the procedures of the various arbitration tribunals and what to expect when actually involved in the arbitration process. It then discusses international dispute resolution systems and organizations. The last chapter deals with other alternative dispute resolution mechanisms. Finally, source material concerning arbitration of intellectual property disputes is provided in the appendices, including sample arbitration clauses used in intellectual property contracts and arbitration submissions in the past. 

This book is the culmination of several years of effort by the Arbitration Committee of The New York Patent, Trademark and Copyright Law Association, Inc.

The committee was reac-

Preface ix tivated in 1982, after several years of dormancy, in response to the renewed interest in alternatives outside the normal courts for resolution of intellectual property disputes. 

At its first organizational meeting, John Pegram, one of the directors of the Assocation and a person keenly interested in alternative methods to resolve disputes, suggested that a guide to arbitration of patent disputes would be a worthwhile project. He even volunteered to write one of the chapters. With this impetus, the committee undertook the project with extreme enthusiasm.

Over the ensuing years, many members of the committee gave unstintingly of their time in the best tradition of service by the bar: Walter Kehm, who was one of the early enthusiastic supporters of the project and who wrote the original organizational outline of the guide. The authors of the various chapters, which were written at various times over the last few years, are- 

Russell J. Juten, Richard E. Lerner, John B. Pegram, and Marvin

L. Soffen (Chapter 1); John B. Pegram (Chapters 2 and 3); Michael Hollering, as edited by John B. Pegram (Chapter 4); Robert H.

We also want to express our appreciation for the support given to this project by the American Arbitration Association, the Board of Directors of the New York Patent, Trademark and Copyright Law Assocation, Inc., all the members of the Arbitration Committee since its reactivation, and The Bureau of 

National Affairs, Inc., and its editors.

We want to make separate note of the aid given by the Center for Public Policy which provided much of the information necessary to prepare Chapter 11 on mini-trials. 

We hope all of this effort will be of use to the profession. 

In this same vein, we would like to update this book in further editions as experience grows with alternative methods of dispute resolution. Your suggestions, particularly as to information not here included, are welcome. 

THOMAS L. CREEL Chairman Arbitration Committee NYPTCLA October 1987

CHAPTER 

Background and Policy

A. Background to Patent Arbitration Statute as The American Arbitration Association defines arbitration "the submission of a disagreement to one or more impartial persons with the understanding that the parties will abide by the arbitrator's decision. Because the decision is binding, arbitration differs from mediation or conciliation where the third party is authorized only to bring the parties together to discuss settlement; it also differs from fact-finding, where an impartial party studies the situation and makes a report. In most instances, arbitrators' decisions are private and only of interest to the parties involved. Few awards must come to the attention of the courts. "Arbitration is a system created by the parties themselves. 

When they have worked hard to settle a dispute but have failed, they can submit the remaining issues to arbitration. Negotiations already may have narrowed their differences and the negotiations can 'control' the remaining issues to be submitted." 

All today might well accept such voluntary dispute resolution as a most worthwhile endeavor. It was not always so. 

For many years, arbitration was viewed with suspicion and looked upon as an attempt to oust courts of their rightful duties of deciding controversies between parties. The fact that such proceedings were conducted in private was suspect, and courts initially refused to enforce an arbitrator's award. It was not until the 1920s that arbitration came of age. Then, in 1925, the 

U.S. Arbitration Act was passed, which is applicable to most 1

Ch. 1 

 business controversies. Later, in 1955, the Uniform Arbitration 

Act was promulgated as a model uniform state law. Today, arbitration is widely used and the courts, with their ever burgeoning caseload, look most favorably on this and many other alternative methods of dispute resolution. 

Why the widespread use of arbitration? According to the experts, many businesses prefer to have their disagreements handled by experts; simplicity is an attraction, as are speed of the proceedings, privacy, informality, nonappealability of awards, and the lack of need to have a company's funds and executives tied up for long periods of time.

Indeed, there are whole industries today where the normal mode of dispute resolution is through arbitration not the courts. 

This has occurred, for example, in the construction industry, the textile/garment industry, international trade areas, as well as in labor disputes.

While the U.S. Arbitration Act was intended to overcome state statutes which had restricted or prevented enforcement of agreements to arbitrate involving transactions in commerce,¹ there were lingering reservations regarding arbitration generally and on "policy" grounds in several areas of law. For example, in Wilko v. Swan,2 an antitrust case, the Supreme Court in 1953 refused to permit arbitration of a question under §14 of the Securities Act of 1933.

A shift in the Supreme Court's attitude toward arbitration was indicated by its 1974 decision in Scherk v. Alberto-Culver Co.3 

That case permitted arbitration of a question under the same section as Wilko. Although the Scherk majority took care to distinguish the facts from Wilko on the basis that an international

No. 255, 80th Cong., 1st Sess., reprinted in 1947 U.S. CODE CONG. & AD. NEWS 1515, 1515. Background and Policy agreement was involved, the dissent construed the decision as overruling Wilko.5

The recent decisions of the Supreme Court have been clearly pro-arbitration. While noting that the "question of public policy is ultimately one for resolution by the courts," the Court declared in 1983 that a public policy which would bar enforcement of an arbitration agreement "must be well defined and dominant, and is to be ascertained by reference to the laws and legal precedents and not from general considerations of supposed public interest.' "8 The most significant recent Supreme Court arbitration decision, however, is Mitsubishi Motors

Corp. v. Soler Chrysler-Plymouth, Inc., in which the Court referred to "a strong presumption in favor of enforcement of freely negotiated contractual choice-of-forum provisions *** by the emphatic federal policy in favor of arbitral dispute resolution."'9 

Although the 5-3 decision, that arbitration in Japan of U.S. antitrust issues pursuant to an automobile dealership agreement was not barred on policy grounds, was expressly limited to situations involving an international agreement, the Court confessed in dicta to skepticism as to certain aspects of the 

American Safety doctrine¹0 and generally praised arbitration.

B. Passage of Patent Arbitration Statute 

The same trend of early hostility to arbitration was evident in the patent area. There had been arbitration over the years of some types of patent disputes, but mainly not concerning patent validity. For example, the question of whether a specific product was within the scope of royalty provisions of a license

Some courts had held, however, that the question of patent validity is so vested with the public interest (as was the case with antitrust disputes) that the parties could not agree to take that question out of the federal court system.¹¹ Beckman Instru ments, Inc. v. Technical Development Corp. is representative: 

"We are in accord with the district court's view that such questions are inappropriate for arbitration proceedings and should be decided by a court of law, given the great public interest in challenging invalid patents As the district court said: 'The complex principles of patent law which a court must consider and apply when deciding issues of validity and infringement, affect important questions of public policy and public rights. In considering the validity of patent claims, a court makes decisions crucial not only to the parties involved, but of vital importance to the public generally." //12 Against this background, several interested members of the bar and private industry proposed a law to make clear that patent validity questions could be arbitrated. President Reagan, on the signing of the bill containing the statute confirming the arbitrability of patent disputes, 35 U.S.C. §294, stated: "A major deterrent to using the patent system, especially by small businesses and independent inventors, is the inordinately high cost of patent litigation. This bill authorizes voluntary arbitration of patent validity and infringement disputes. This will not only improve the patent system and encourage innovation, it will relieve the burden on the federal courts. Development of new technology is a vital ingredient in my administration's plan for economic recovery. This measure is a significant step in this process."'13 

Section 294(a) provides: "A contract involving a patent or any right under a patent may contain a provision requiring arbitration of any dispute relating to patent validity or infringement arising under the contract. In the absence of such a provision, the parties to an existing patent

Background and Policy validity or infringement dispute may agree in writing to settle such dispute by arbitration. Any such provision or agreement shall be valid, irrevocable, and enforceable, except for any grounds that exist at law or in equity for revocation of a contract." 

C. Arbitration of Patent Disputes Consistent With Public Policy 

The arbitration of patent disputes is consistent with the broad mandates of the courts in construing other aspects of patent law. For example, in Lear v. Adkins,¹4 the Supreme Court held that it would be inconsistent with federal patent and public policy to estop a licensee from contesting the validity of a patent. Arbitration of patent validity under 35 U.S.C. §294 appears consistent with this principle. If, in a license agreement, there is a provision to arbitrate patent disputes, such agreement simply sets forth the forum in which the patent issues are to be decided and should not in any sense be construed as an agreement not to contest validity. Indeed, the simpler and less expensive procedure of arbitration should serve to provide a licensee with a more viable opportunity to contest validity. Thus, arbitration before a technically qualified arbitrator or panel is consistent with and would further the public policy behind Lear. 

In Blonder-Tongue Laboratories, Inc. v. University of Illinois

Foundation, 15 the Supreme Court decided that a judgment of invalidity in a suit against one accused infringer can accrue to the benefit of a later defendant, unless the patent owner shows that he did not have a fair opportunity, procedurally, substantivally, and evidentially, to litigate his patent claim in the suit leading to that judgment. A question arises as to whether, in an arbitration proceeding resulting in a finding of patent invalidity, an estoppel would result against the patentee with respect to any future assertion of its patent. If not, the Blonder- 

Tongue policy could be undermined. No case has yet answered this question. While 35 U.S.C. §294(c) provides that an "award by an arbitrator shall be final and binding between the parties to the arbitration but shall have no force or effect on any other person," this provision is apparently intended to permit any third party to contest patent validity where the patent was held 14395 U.S. 653, 162 USPQ 1 (1969). 

15402 U.S. 313, 169 USPQ 513 (1971).

Ch. 1 

 valid in an earlier arbitration. However, a patentee who had chosen arbitration, assuming he had a fair opportunity to pursue his claim, could be arguably bound by such arbitration, as if the patent had been litigated in a district court. 

Moreover, in an arbitration, a party who contested validity and lost the arbitration could take advantage of a later court determination of invalidity or uneforceability. Section 294(c) provides: "The parties to an arbitration may agree that in the event a patent which is the subject matter of an award is subsequently determined to be invalid or unenforceable in a judgment rendered by a court to [of] competent jurisdiction from which no appeal can or has been taken, such award may be modified by any court of competent jurisdiction upon application by any party to the arbitration. Any such modification shall govern the rights and obligations between such parties from the date of such modification."

There appears to be no reason why the change of forum from a court to an arbitration panel should affect the reasoning of Blonder-Tongue. As the arbitrators, presumably, would be from a technically competent panel, they should be well equipped to provide the patentee with an opportunity to fully and fairly present his case. Indeed in many cases the arbitrators would be technically trained and thus in certain areas might be more competent to decide the case than a federal court judge. Assuming arbitration is encouraged so as to remove cases (particularly complex patent cases) from the courts, the same recognition and the same benefits and risks to all parties should be urged to apply as in a court adjudication. 

Furthermore, 35 U.S.C. §294 and antitrust policy should not be in conflict. Section (b) provides "the defenses provided for under Section 282 of this title shall be considered by the arbitrator if raised by any party to the proceeding." Under 35 

U.S.C. §282, defenses to a claim for patent infringement include noninfringement, absence of liability for infringement, and unenforceability. Defenses relating to unenforceability or unclean hands are often based on facts (for example, fraud or misuse) that might support or give rise to a claim under the antitrust laws, if other elements, such as a strong position in the relevant market, are present. In such situations it would appear that the arbitrator can consider and rule on the defenses

Ch. 7 

Background and Policy presented, consistent with the recent Supreme Court pronouncements encouraging arbitration of antitrust disputes. 

In summary, the enactment of 35 U.S.C. §294 is consistent with public policy challenging invalid patents and is not inconsistent with Lear v. Adkins or Blonder-Tongue. The statute permits a party to raise any defense available under 35 U.S.C. 

§282. The notice provision of 35 U.S.C. §294(d) provides that any award made as a result of the arbitration becomes part of the public record.

D. Alternative Dispute Resolution Systems and Forums 

The public benefit of arbitration is the providing of an alternative from the time-consuming and technical complexities of patent litigation and thereby saving substantial court time and litigants' expense. Lately, arbitration has become recognized as only one of a number of ways to resolve disputes quickly, inexpensively, and flexibly. All of these methods have become known generally as Alternative Dispute Resolution systems (ADRs) and, in addition to arbitration, include mediation, fact-finding, conciliation, mini-trials, and "rent-a-judge." Each of these systems generally involves the use of some sort of neutral third party to help in resolving the dispute and are treated later in this book. (See Chapter 11.)

There are a number of arbitration organizations that provide services to the parties. The American Arbitration Association (AAA) is probably the best known in the United States. 

Through its 31 offices, the AAA administers cases under its various rules, provides hearing rooms, and has an experienced staff to assist with the mechanics and logistics of an arbitration. 

It provides the parties with lists of qualified arbitrators from which they can mutually choose those who will decide their dispute. As a direct result of the passage of $294, the AAA, with the aid of an advisory committee of experts, promulgated rules for patent arbitration and established a blue ribbon panel of arbitrators with vast technical and patent law backgrounds. (See Appendix B.)

While many agencies, such as the AAA, administer both domestic and international arbitrations (as discussed in Chapter 10), there are arbitral institutions which operate exclusively in the international field. Through treaties and international con-

Ch. 1 

 ventions, arbitration agreements and awards may be enforced in most major trading countries.

E. Use of Patent Arbitration 

A patent arbitration is made public only if there has been an award and the notice has been given to the Commissioner of Patents (35 U.S.C. §§294(d) and (e)). Unfortunately, the Pat ent Office keeps no records from which the total number of reported arbitration awards can be determined. This is because the Patent Office merely places a notice of award in the file jacket of the patent in issue, but makes no other record of the award. Statistics as to the active number of arbitrations of patent disputes are therefore not available. 

AAA statistics for the years 1985 and 1986 indicate that 24 cases involving patent royalties had been filed under the Commercial Arbitration Rules, as opposed to the Patent Arbitration Rules. Presumably these cases did not raise issues of validity and infringement, and thus no notice to the Patent Office was required under §294. The statistics also reveal that three cases were filed in 1985 and 1986 with the AAA under its Patent Arbitration Rules. Interestingly, as very often occurs in arbitration, these cases were settled and there were no awardsand nothing to file with the Commissioner. 

However, there are many indications that arbitration will be more extensively used in the future. Many articles such as 

Paul D. Carmichael's The Arbitration of Patent Disputes, 16 and John W. Schlicher's The Patent Arbitration Law: A New Procedure for Resolving Patent Infringement Disputes, 17 have been published. 

The American Arbitration Association has held seminars in New York and on the West Coast to discuss the system with patent attorneys. Many patent law associations have held seminars or dedicated programs to updating the patent bar on this alternative method of dispute settlement. 

Furthermore, a recent survey of members of the New York 

Patent, Trademark and Copyright Law Association shows that many members of the patent bar are routinely including arbitration clauses of varying scope and detail when preparing license agreements. This is particularly true in licenses with foreign 

1638 ARB. J. 3 (1983). 

1740 ARB. J. 7 (1985).

Ch. 9 

Background and Policy parties. Such "new" licenses may not have had sufficient time for disputes to have arisen and to have been submitted under the patent arbitration rules, though eventually they may appear in the statistics. Furthermore, patent arbitration with regard to foreign patents often takes place before the International Chamber of Commerce, or there could be other alternative dispute resolution techniques adopted which would not have to be reported since they are not strictly arbitrations. Thus, there is no way of determining accurately just how much alternative dispute resolution is taking place. It can be stated with a fair degree of assurance, however, that ADR is routinely looked at now when a dispute arises and that its use will increase in the future.

CHAPTER The Patent Arbitration Law* 

In 1982, the Patent Act was amended to add §294, which (1) stated the express intent of Congress that parties be able to arbitrate questions of patent validity and infringement; (2) included certain express provisions regarding such arbitrationsnotably a provision for public notice of arbitration awards; and (3) referred to the U.S. Arbitration Act as governing to the extent not inconsistent with §294.¹ In 1984, §294 was extended to cover arbitration of interference disputes.2 

Section 294 clarified the availability of arbitration to resolve patent validity and infringement disputes, but it also raised a number of questions regarding patent arbitrations and the jurisdiction of the courts. At almost the same time that the U.S. 

Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit came into existence with broad and exclusive jurisdiction over appeals in various actions arising under Acts of Congress relating to patents,³ §294 raised the possibility of increased numbers of patent arbitrations with awards confirmed and reviewed by a variety of federal and state courts.

This chapter reviews the amendments of the Patent Act to expressly permit patent arbitration between citizens of the United *This chapter is a current version of John Pegram's article published in 11 Am. Pat. 

L.A. Q.J. 274, “"An Analysis and Explanation of the Statutes Now Affecting Patent 

Arbitration" (1983). 

135 U.S.C. §294, 96 Stat. 322 (1982). 

235 U.S.C. §135(d), Pub. L. No. 98-622, §105, 98 Stat. 3385 (1984). 

328 U.S.C. §1295, 96 Stat. 37 (1982). See generally The Court of Appeals for the Federal 

Circuit, 10 AM. PAT. L.A. Q.J. 4 (1982). 10

Ch. 11 

The Patent Arbitration Law 

States (§294 of the Patent Act)* and patent interference arbitra tion (§135(d) of the Patent Act).5 A. Patent Disputes Are Arbitrable Section 294(a) states: 

"A contract involving a patent or any right under a patent may contain a provision requiring arbitration of any dispute relating to patent validity or infringement arising under the contract. In the absence of such a provision, the parties to an existing patent validity or infringement dispute may agree in writing to settle such dispute by arbitration. Any such provision or agreement shall be valid, irrevocable and enforceable, except for any grounds that exist at law or in equity for revocation of a contract." 

The first two sentences of §294(a) are permissive: arbitration of patent validity and infringement disputes is permitted by agreement between the parties. Once such an agreement is reached, the last sentence of this section mandates that the arbitration provisions be treated as valid, irrevocable, and enforceable, subject to the usual rules for revocation of contracts. 

Section 294(a) generally parallels §2 of the U.S. Arbitration Act, Title 9 of the U.S. Code. While §2 refers to arbitration of a controversy arising out of a contract evidencing a transaction in commerce or out of the transaction itself, §294(a) refers to arbitration of a limited class of disputes-patent validity or infringement disputes—arising out of a contract or an existing dispute involving a patent or any right under a patent. The last sentence of §294(a), regarding validity of arbitration agreements, is copied from the last portion of §2. The clear implication is that Congress meant what it said in the U.S. Arbitration 

Act: agreements to arbitrate shall be enforced, subject only to the rules of contract law.7

There is one substantial difference in structure between §294(a) and §2, relating to oral agreements to arbitrate. Section 

2 provides that written agreements to arbitrate disputes "in- 

435 U.S.C. §294. 

535 U.S.C. §135(d). 

"Hereafter referred to as "Title 9." Sections of that act are hereafter referred to by their number, e.g., §2. 

7Care should be taken, especially with governmental entities, to be sure that the party has the capacity to enter into an agreement to arbitrate.

Ch. 2 

 volving commerce" shall be valid, irrevocable, and enforceable. Under §294(a), an agreement to arbitrate an existing dispute must be in writing, but an agreement to arbitrate a future dispute as to patent validity or infringement need not be in writing. Although one can settle an existing dispute by an enforceable oral agreement, there no doubt will be litigation over whether an otherwise enforceable oral agreement to arbitrate an existing patent dispute is enforceable because of these differences between §294(a) and §2. This question, in turn, is likely to be decided by a judicial determination of whether §294 is mandatory or permissive.10

If an oral agreement to arbitrate future patent validity or infringement disputes is otherwise enforceable under contract law, then §294 and thus Title 9 to the extent it is not inconsistent with §294-would apply to the arbitration provisions, although Title 9 would not apply directly to an oral agreement to arbitrate.11 

One peculiarity of §294(a) is that it refers only to patent validity or infringement disputes. Disputes under a patent license, such as accounting and payment disputes, may not involve questions of patent law at all. These are outside the scope of §294, although they may still be arbitrable under Title 9.

The Patent Arbitration Law licensed patent is not a question of patent infringement. Arbitration of that question would also be outside the provisions of §294.13 On the other hand, if the same or a similar question was raised in terms of the scope of a patent claim, the question might involve a dispute as to validity of the claim and thus be within §294. The principal significance of the possibility that patent license arbitrations are outside §294 is the possible effect on jurisdiction of the courts in related proceedings, 14 and the fact that no notice need be given to the Commissioner of Patents and Trademarks of awards in such arbitrations. 15 Of course, by raising an issue of patent validity, a party could bring the arbitration within §294 and the notice requirements would apply to at least that portion of the award bearing on infringement and validity. 

One intriguing question, which has yet to be answered in a patent arbitration context, is whether the arbitrators retain jurisdiction if a party becomes involved in bankruptcy proceedings. Ordinarily, the decision of whether or not to stay bankruptcy proceedings pending arbitration is at the discretion of the Bankruptcy Judge. 16 The district court in Mitsubishi Motors 

Corp. v. Soler Chrysler-Plymouth, Inc., faced this question in an international contractual context; it withdrew the proceedings from Bankruptcy Court and ordered the parties to proceed with arbitration without delay.17

The first sentence of §294(b) directly refers to some, but not all, of the subjects covered by Title 9: "Arbitration of such disputes, awards by arbitrators and confirmation of awards shall be governed by title 9, United States Code, to the extent such title is not inconsistent with this section."19

At first glance, it would appear from this statement that Title 9 would deal with arbitration, awards, and confirmation of awards. That is not the case. Title 9 says very little about the conduct of arbitration itself, the most important provision regarding conduct of arbitration being the procedure for compelling the attendance of witnesses, and very little is said about awards.20

Rather, Title 9 is concerned primarily with federal court procedures for dealing with questions involving arbitrations.21

Title 9 applies to any arbitration involving a transaction affecting commerce that comes before a state or federal court. 22 For that reason, there was no need for §294(b) to refer to such matters as staying court proceedings pending an arbitration23 or the procedure for handling applications to a court in connection with an arbitration.24

2. All Patent Defenses Under §282 May Be Considered in an Arbitration 

One of the reasons for the past restriction of arbitration of patent issues was the fear that an arbitrator might decide something that, for policy reasons, should only be decided by the courts. Some courts, and staff members of the Department of

Justice's Antitrust Division, indicated a belief that patent issues were too important to arbitrate.27 Patent enforceability issues raised even more fears, as the underlying facts might also relate to a claim under the antitrust laws which was not considered arbitrable. 28

Most legislative proposals for arbitration of patent validity and infringement issues prior to enactment of §294 did not mention the question of which defenses would be considered. 

Thus, under those proposals, there would probably have been litigation over the scope of patent arbitration, resulting in a hobbled patent arbitration procedure. that:

Section 294(b), which squarely faces this issue, provides "In any such arbitration proceeding, the defenses provided for under §282 of this title shall be considered by the arbitrator if raised by any party to the proceeding."29

Section 282 of the Patent Act30 provides for a full range of defenses to a patent: "(1) Noninfringement, absence of liability for infringement, or unenforceability; "(2) Invalidity of the patent or any claim in suit on any ground

Many of the potential defenses to a patent, such as misuse, are based on facts which may give rise to an antitrust claim. 

While an arbitration may consider these issues as defenses to a patent suit under the auspices of §282(4) of the Patent Act, it remains unclear whether those same issues can be arbitrated offensively in support of a claim of antitrust violation. Dicta in the Supreme Court's recent Mitsubishi opinion³2 strongly suggests that such claims are arbitrable; however, the holding in 

Mitsubishi was limited to international agreements subject to the Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards.33 The lower courts, however, are still split over whether antitrust claims are arbitrable. 34 

The provisions of §294 are limited to "any such arbitration proceeding," presumably meaning any arbitration by agreement of a dispute relating to patent validity or infringement. 

Thus, there is a question whether or not the literal language of §294 would require, for example, enforcement of an arbitrator's award regarding an enforceability defense raised in response to a royalty dispute under a patent license. While §294 does not expressly permit arbitration in that case, in view of the policy indicated by the enactment of §294 and the Supreme

The Patent Arbitration Law

While §294(b) requires that a defense under the Patent Act be considered if raised by a party, it neither expressly authorizes an arbitrator to consider a defense not raised by a party nor does it bar such consideration. The arbitrator's freedom to consider such matters depends primarily on the scope of the matter submitted for arbitration.35 Also of significance on this question is whether or not the arbitrator is required to give reasons for the award.36 If there are no reasons comparable to a district judge's findings of fact and conclusions of law, it, arguably, would be more unfair for an arbitrator to consider and decide the matter on a defense not raised by a party than for a judge to do so.

C. The Arbitration Award 1. Effect of an Award 

Section 294(c) incorporates several concepts, all involving the finality of the arbitration award. The first sentence of the section provides: "An award by an arbitrator shall be final and binding between the parties to the arbitration but shall have no force or effect on any other person." 1137

This renders the award free from review or appeal, except for possible modification or variation pursuant to §10 or §11 of Title 

9, or by prior agreement of the parties. If the parties do not intend their agreement to be "final and binding,"38 or if they 35 The arbitrator's authority is limited to that conferred by the arbitration agreement. 

See 9 U.S.C. §10 (arbitrator's exceeding powers grounds for vacating award). 

36 The American Arbitration Association generally has suggested that no reasons be given for awards in arbitrations. See, e.g., AMERICAN ARBITRATION ASSOCIATION, A GUIDE FOR COMMERCIAL ARBITRATORS 16 (1983); Hoellering, "The Patent Arbitration Rules of the American Arbitration Association,” in Patent Arbitration: What You Need to Know— A Walk Through Demonstration, 1985 A.B.A. SEC. LITIGATION 15 n.33. 

3735 U.S.C. §294(c).

Ch. 2 

 wish a reviewing court to have greater power of review,39 they should so state clearly in their agreement. 

The "final and binding between the parties" language appears to set the scene for the subsequent provision that the award "shall have no force or effect on any other person." That provision requires analysis in three principal respects: (1) the meaning of "any other person"; (2) the effect of confirmation of the award; and (3) the effect on the patent owner. "Any other person" could mean any person not actually a party to the arbitration agreement, but that is probably too restrictive an interpretation. Heirs, successors, and assigns, and the like, specified in the agreement, should also have the obligations and benefits of the arbitration award. Clearly, the intent of the "any other person" language was to bar an adverse effect on unrelated third parties. But what of a beneficial effect on such parties, or an effect on other persons in privity with a party to the agreement? These matters will require resolution by the courts.

Title 9 provides that an award may be confirmed by a judgment of a court if the parties so provide in their agreement.40 It further provides: "The judgment shall be docketed as if it was rendered in an action. "The judgment so entered shall have the same force and effect, in all respects as, and be subject to all the provisions of law relating to, a judgment in an action; and it may be enforced as if it had been rendered in an action in the court in which it is entered."'41

Thus, in the ordinary case under Title 9, a confirmed award would have the full res judicata and collateral estoppel effect of a judgment of the confirming court. Logically, res judicata should apply to such awards in patent cases, and they have been given that effect in other fields. 42 Collateral estoppel effect

Section 294(b), however, provides that "confirmation of awards shall be governed by title 9 *** to the extent such title is not inconsistent with this section." The question then is whether or not the usual full effect of a confirmed award is inconsistent with §294(b)'s proviso that the award "shall have no force or effect on any other person." If the court actually reviewed the arbitrator's decision, as is done in the case of proposed findings of fact and recommendations by a U.S. magistrate, there certainly would be justification for having confirmation of the award expand the effect of the award to the full scope of a judgment after trial. That, however, is not the procedure. The award of an arbitrator is not necessarily accompanied by findings of fact and conclusions of law,45 unlike the report or judgment of a magistrate.46 Under Title 9, the court does not review the decision at the time of confirmation, but must grant the order confirming the award unless the award is vacated, modified, or corrected.47 In view of this and the apparent legislative intent to limit the scope of arbitration awards involving patent validity and infringement, it seems likely that the "any other person" restriction of §294(c) will be expressly, or at least by implication, included in confirmed awards made under $294.

Section 294(c) may not restrict the effect of an arbitration award or a confirmed award upon the patent owner. In particular, if an award held one or more claims of a patent invalid

2. Modification of an Award

The second part of §294(c) permits the parties to an arbitration to agree that, if the patent is later held invalid or unenforceable, their arbitration award may be modified. "The parties to an arbitration may agree that in the event a patent which is the subject matter of an award is subsequently determined to be invalid or unenforceable in a judgment rendered by a court of competent jurisdiction from which no appeal can or has been taken, such award may be modified by any court of competent jurisdiction upon application by any party to the arbitration. Any such modification shall govern the rights and obligations between the parties from the date of such modification."50 

The right to modification provided by this subsection is not automatic; it must be included in an agreement and is prospective only.

The modification provision of §294(c) is a valuable suggestion for consideration by drafters of patent arbitration agreements, but it raises again the question of whether the provisions of §294 are mandatory or permissive. Although not yet decided by the courts, the modification provision probably does not preclude parties from agreeing to refund some or all past payments if a patent is later held invalid. It will probably be some 

48 The sense of ABA-PTC Committee 402 (Arbitration and Other Alternative Pro cedures), expressed in its 1983 Report, was that giving a collateral estoppel effect to arbitration awards would be contrary to the intent of Congress. 1983 Committee Reports, A.B.A. SEC. PAT., TRADEMARK AND COPYRIGHT LAW 201, 202 ("Gray Book"). 

49 On the other hand, a failure to state that the patent is valid and infringed may affect the validity of the confirmed award as a judgment. See generally ch. 3 at nn. 

34-39, infra. 

5035 U.S.C. §294(c).

Ch. 21 

The Patent Arbitration Law time before other questions are clearly resolved by the courts; for example, is a court proceeding the only way in which an award can be modified to take into account a subsequent determination of patent invalidity or unenforceability? What if the award has been confirmed? The permissive wording of the provision suggests that modification can be sought by other means, such as further arbitration pursuant to terms of the original agreement.

It is likely that §294(c) did not expressly permit arbitration on the modification issue because the question of modification was considered a simple one. For example, consider an arbitration award made with respect to infringement of a single patent. Later, a final judgment is entered finding the patent to be invalid. Modification of the first award would be quite simple, and there would be no need to prolong proceedings by first conducting a new arbitration regarding the modification and then confirming the award. But that is not the only way in which modification questions may arise. What if the original award is not specific as to which claims are infringed and only one of the claims of the patent is later found invalid? If the intent of the parties was to arbitrate infringement questions, would it not be better to turn again to an arbitrator rather than "any court of competent jurisdiction"?51

D. Notice to the Commissioner 

Section 294(d) provides for notice to the Commissioner of Patents and Trademarks of an arbitrator's award: "When an award is made by an arbitrator, the patentee, his assignee or licensee shall give notice thereof in writing to the Commissioner. There shall be a separate notice prepared for each patent involved in such proceeding. Such notice shall set forth the names and addresses of the parties, the name of the inventor, and the name of the patent owner, shall designate the number of the patent, and shall contain a copy of the award. If an award is modified by a court, the party requesting such modification shall give notice to the Commissioner. The Commissioner shall, upon receipt of either notice, enter the same in the record of the 

51 That court may be a state court. See Moses H. Cone Memorial Hospital v. Mercury 

Constr. Corp., 460 Ú.S. 1 (1983), quoted, infra, ch. 3 at n.5. See ch. 3 at nn.62 et seq., infra, regarding subject matter jurisdiction.

Ch. 2 

 prosecution of such patent. If the required notice is not filed with the Commissioner, any party to the proceeding may provide such notice to the Commissioner." 

Section 294(e) contains a related provision: "The award shall be unenforceable until the notice required by subsection (d) is received by the Commissioner."'52

The Patent and Trademark Office rules repeat the substance of subsections (d) and (e) in a differently phrased form.53 

While §294(d) expressly refers to filing a notice of modification of an award by a court, it does not refer to or expressly permit filing of a notice of the confirmation, vacating, or correction of an award. "Confirm," "vacate," "modify," and "correct" are terms of art used in Title 9, which is incorporated by reference in §294.54 No doubt, when referring to modification of the award, the drafters of this section had in mind the modification possible under §294(c) when a patent is later determined to be invalid or unenforceable, and notices of that type can and should be filed. The situation is not quite so clear with respect to filing notices of modification, vacating, or correction under Title 9.55 Broadly, the word "modification" could be construed to include all of these, but-if the drafters had intended to include all-why was only one of the Title 9 terms used in §294(d)?

Perhaps the various actions a district court can take with respect to an award were not included in §294(d) because of a belief that they would be the subject of a notice by a court clerk pursuant to existing §290 of the Patent Act. That section provides: "The clerks of the courts of the United States, within one month after the filing of an action under this title shall give notice thereof in writing to the Commissioner. * * * Within one month after the decision is rendered or a judgment is issued the clerk of the court shall give notice thereof to the Commissioner. The 

Commissioner shall, on receipt of such notices, enter the same in the file of such patent.' #156

Two principal questions arise regarding the relevance of this 

5235 U.S.C. §294(e). 

5337 C.F.R. §1.335. 

54 See 9 U.S.C. §§9-11. 

55 Id. 

5635 U.S.C. §290.

Ch. 23 

The Patent Arbitration Law section to notices of actions taken with respect to arbitration awards: (1) who are the clerks of the courts of the United States; and (2) is a Title 9 proceeding to confirm, vacate, modify, or correct an arbitration award "an action under this title" (Title 

35 of the Patent Act)? 

The phrase "clerks of the courts of the United States” in $290 seems to refer to clerks of federal courts, rather than clerks of all courts within the United States.57 The reference is in the 

Patent Act, a context in which the only jurisdiction at the time of enactment was in the federal courts. 58 Thus, there apparently is no obligation on state court clerks to file notices of confirmation, vacating, modification, or correction of arbitration awards under $290.

It seems clear that a Title 9 proceeding was not considered an action arising under the Patent Act at the time §290 was enacted, and it is unlikely that the Patent Act's drafters would have expected the court clerks to give notice of arbitration proceedings to the Patent and Trademark Office. Furthermore, the phrase in §290 "under this title" implies a title which creates a cause of action.59

As a practical matter, the Civil Cover Sheet used by the federal district courts provides boxes to indicate what type of action is involved.60 A check adjacent "Patent" on that sheet should trigger the filing of a §290 notice by the court clerk, but a check adjacent "Other Statutory Actions" or some other term is unlikely to do so.

E. Interference Arbitration 

In 1984, the Patent Act was amended to expressly permit arbitration of patent interference disputes.61 Paragraph 135(d) was added to the Act by this amendment: 

57 The phrase "in any of the courts of the United States" in Title 9 was considered "ambiguous" by the Supreme Court in Cone, supra note 51, 460 U.S. at 26 n.34. 

58 See 28 U.S.C. §1338(a) (district courts), former §1498(a) (1948) (U.S. Court of

59 Compare, e.g., 28 U.S.C. §1331 ("The district courts shall have original jurisdiction of all civil actions arising under the Constitution, laws, or treaties of the United States."), §1338(a) ("The district courts shall have original jurisdiction of any civil action arising under any Act of Congress relating to patents * *.") See also 28 U.S.C. §§1337, 1339, 1340. See ch. 3 at n.71 et seq., infra. 

60 Form JS-44 (S.D.N.Y.). 

6135 U.S.C. §135(d), Pub. L. No. 98-622, §105, 98 Stat. 3385 (1984).

Ch. 2 

"Parties to a patent interference, within such time as may be specified by the Commissioner by regulation, may determine such contest or any aspect thereof by arbitration. Such arbitration shall be governed by the provisions of title 9 to the extent such title is not inconsistent with this section. The parties shall give notice of any arbitration award to the Commissioner, and such award shall, as between the parties to the arbitration, be dispositive of the issues to which it relates. The arbitration award shall be unenforceable until such notice is given. Nothing in this subsection shall preclude the Commissioner from determining patentability of the invention involved in the interference."

The amendment provided that it was not to be effective until the Patent and Trademark Office adopted the necessary rules, which became effective May 27, 1987.62 The rules take a restrictive approach to arbitration of interference disputes, in an apparent effort to assure compliance with interference time schedules and to avoid any adverse effect on the determination of patentability, which the statutory amendment and the rules properly reserved to the Commissioner.

CHAPTER General Federal Law Applicable to Patent Arbitration Questions This chapter addresses aspects of the U.S. Arbitration Act and other federal statutes bearing on patent arbitration.

A. The U.S. Arbitration Act 

The U.S. Arbitration Act, which was first enacted in 1925¹ and was codified in Title 9 of the U.S. Code in 1947,2 provides that written agreements to arbitrate controversies involving transactions in commerce are valid, irrevocable, and enforceable. In 1970, chapter 2 regarding the enforcement of the Con vention on Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral 

Awards was added, and the previously existing provisions were designated "General Provisions” and placed in chapter 1.3

The Supreme Court addressed the nature of Title 9 in Moses H. Cone Memorial Hospital v. Mercury Construction Corp., saying:

"The Arbitration Act is something of an anomaly in the field of federal-court jurisdiction. It creates a body of federal substantive law establishing and regulating the duty to honor an agreement to arbitrate, yet it does not create any independent federalquestion jurisdiction. *** Nevertheless, although enforcement of the Act is left in large part to the state courts, it nevertheless represents federal policy to be vindicated by the federal courts where otherwise appropriate."5

Since many of the provisions of Title 9 have already been discussed in the preceding chapter, or will be included in the discussion of jurisdiction later in this chapter, the treatment here will be limited to an overview of chapter 1 of Title 96 and mention of some principal provisions of interest in patent arbitrations.6 

1. Scope of Title 9 Section 2 of Title 9 provides that: 

"A written provision in any maritime transaction[7] or a contract evidencing a transaction involving commerce to settle by arbitration a controversy thereafter arising out of such contract or transaction, or the refusal to perform the whole or any part thereof, or an agreement in writing to submit to arbitration an existing controversy arising out of such a contract, transaction or refusal, shall be valid, irrevocable, and enforceable, save upon such grounds as exist at law or in equity for the revocation of any contract."'8 

According to the Supreme Court in Cone: "The effect of the section [2] is to create a body of federal substantive law of arbitrability, applicable to any arbitration agree ment within the coverage of the Act. *** The Arbitration Act establishes that, as a matter of federal law, any doubts concerning the scope of arbitrable issues should be resolved in favor of arbitration, whether the problem at hand is the construction of

Furthermore, there are still questions as to whether all antitrust and securities law claims are arbitrable, and whether seeking court determination of such claims waives arbitration.11

In construing an arbitration agreement, the arbitrator should determine the validity and enforceability of the agreement when there is no issue as to the validity of the arbitration clause itself. 12 Even questions of fraud in inducing the agreement may be arbitrable under Title 9, so long as there is no question of fraudulent inducement of the arbitration clause itself. 13

Ch. 3 

 2. Staying Litigation 

Section 3 provides that "any of the courts of the United States," upon application by a party, shall stay, pending arbitration, proceedings on any issue found referable to arbitration, providing that the party seeking the stay is not in default in proceeding with the arbitration. 16 In Cone, the Supreme Court interpreted §3 as obliging state courts, as well as federal courts, to grant stays of court proceedings pending arbitration, noting: "Although §3 refers ambiguously to a suit 'in any of the courts of the United States,' the state courts have almost unanimously recognized that the stay provision of §3 applies to suits in state as well as federal courts, requiring them to issue the same speedy relief when a dispute is referable to arbitration. * * *

This is necessary to carry out Congress's intent to mandate enforcement of all covered arbitration agreements; Congress can hardly have meant that an agreement to arbitrate can be enforced against a party who attempts to litigate an arbitrable dispute in federal court, but not against one who sues on the same dispute in state court."'17 

Note that §3 permits "a party" to apply for a stay pending arbitration. Although the later reference to "the party seeking the stay" in §3 is in the context of a party not in default in proceeding with arbitration, "a party" who may seek a stay of court proceedings has been broadly construed to include a party to either the arbitration or the litigation. 18

Whether an interlocutory appeal can be taken from an order refusing to stay litigation pending arbitration is discussed below. 

3. Compelling Arbitration 

Section 4 provides a means to compel arbitration pursuant to an agreement by a proceeding "in any United States district court which, save for [the written arbitration agreement], would have jurisdiction * * * in a civil action *** of the subject matter of a suit arising out of the controversy between the parties

***"'19 While the express language of §4 expressly refers only to U.S. district courts, at least two courts have interpreted it as applying also to state trial courts.20

Under §4 the court must determine whether the making of the agreement for arbitration or the failure to comply therewith is at issue. The main type of failure to comply with the arbitration agreement is waiver by the party seeking arbitration. 

The cases and literature on that subject are too extensive to summarize here. Not only does a failure to seek arbitration constitute waiver, so too can an unexplained delay, an admission of judicial jurisdiction in an answer, participation in discovery, and moving for summary judgment.21 On the other hand, an amended complaint which "significantly broadened the focus of the litigation" led to the grant of an arbitration motion after nearly four years of litigation in one recent case.22

If there is an issue of the existence of an arbitration agreement or failure to comply with one, the court is to proceed summarily to its trial. There is an express right to trial by jury of such issues. 23 Once it is determined by court or jury that an agreement for arbitration was made in writing24 and that there is a default thereunder, the court is required to make an order directing the parties to proceed with arbitration in accordance with the terms of the agreement.25

4. Court Appointment of Arbitrators

Section 5 provides for the appointment of arbitrators if no method for doing so is provided in the arbitration agreement or for some other reason there is a lapse in naming an arbitrator or filling a vacancy.27 This section, unlike §§3, 4, 7, 10, and 11, refers to action by the court without express or implied limitation to a U.S. court.

The various rules of the American Arbitration Association and other groups usually provide for appointment of the arbitrator when the parties have not appointed one and have not provided a means for appointing one in their agreement.28 

5. Arbitration Applications Treated as Motions Section 6 states that: 

"Any application to the court hereunder shall be made and heard in the manner provided by law for making and hearing of motions, except as otherwise herein expressly provided."29

This section is procedural.30 The type of court is not specified; it could be a state or federal court. Several other sections contain provisions regarding the procedure for handling applications under Title 9, especially §§9-13.

General Federal Law 6. Compelling Testimony and Documentary Production Section 7 is the most significant provision of Title 9 con cerning the conduct of arbitration proceedings. It provides that arbitrators may summon any person to appear before them as a witness and to bring "any book, record, document, or paper which may be deemed material as evidence in the case," and establishes the subpoena procedure. Section 7 places the power of the U.S. district courts behind such subpoenas. "[I]f any person or persons so summoned to testify shall refuse or neglect to obey said summons, upon petition the United States district court for the district in which such arbitrators, or a majority of them, are sitting may compel the attendance of such person or persons before said arbitrator or arbitrators, or punish said person or persons for contempt in the same manner provided by law for securing the attendance of witnesses or their punishment for neglect or refusal to attend in the courts of the 

United States."/31

The subpoena power under §7 does not extend to discovery. 

Discovery, if any, is governed by the rules of the arbitration and not by the Federal Rules of Civil Procedure; however, in a few rare cases, federal courts have permitted discovery in an arbitration under the auspices of the court.32 If discovery is important to the proper resolution of potential disputes, the right to discovery and a delineation of its scope should be included in the arbitration clause. 

7. Admiralty Matters Section 8 permits arbitration proceedings in admiralty mat ters to be begun by a libel and seizure.33

Ch. 3 

 8. Confirmation of Awards 

The arbitrator's judgment is known as an award. Section 

9 provides two procedures for confirmation of arbitration awards.34 The first applies when the parties have agreed to confirmation of the award by a judgment and have specified the court. The other procedure applies when no court has been specified.

In the first case, "at any time within one year after the award is made any party to the arbitration may apply to the court so specified for an order confirming the award, and thereupon the court must grant such an order unless the award is vacated, modified, or corrected as prescribed in sections 10 and 11" of Title 9. The application should be made in the same manner as a motion, in accordance with §6, and the papers specified in §13 should be filed. One caveat, however, is that the court in question must have subject matter jurisdiction, because §9 does not confer such jurisdiction.35

In the second case, where no court has been specified in the parties' agreement, §9 specifies that "such application may be made to the United States court in and for the district within which such award was made." Section 9 provides a procedure for service of notice of the proceeding and thereby obtaining personal jurisdiction of other parties. Again, however, there is à potential problem of obtaining subject matter jurisdiction. 

Since parties cannot by agreement confer subject matter jurisdiction upon a court, the jurisdiction of the intended confirming court should be considered when drafting the arbitration agreement.36 As a district court said, in finding lack of subject matter jurisdiction: "In rendering this decision, the court is not unmindful of the difficulties [applicant] will encounter in its attempts to obtain confirmation of the arbitration awards. Since this court is the 'United States court in and for the district within which such award was made,' 9 U.S.C. §9, an order which effectively divests the court of its jurisdiction over the matter leaves [applicant] 

349 U.S.C. §9. 

35 See text in nn.62 et seq., infra. The mention of "Jurisdiction" in the caption of §9 is apparently a reference to the personal jurisdiction conferred by the second part of that section. If the parties specify the court for confirmation purposes, they have waived any objection to personal jurisdiction and venue. See text at nn.77 et seq., infra. 

36 See text at nn.62 et seq., infra.

Ch. 33 

General Federal Law without an alternative remedial option under the Federal Arbitration Act."37

In most situations where confirmation of an arbitration award by a federal court is not possible, the parties might be able to obtain confirmation from a state court.38 Query, however, whether this is possible in the case of arbitration awards involving patent validity or infringement, in view of the provision of §294(b) that such arbitrations "shall be governed by title 9"?

If the agreement does not specify rules such as those of the AAA, which state that the parties are deemed to have consented to entry of a judgment upon the award, care should be taken to express the parties' intent in the arbitration agreement that a judgment may be entered on the award. Otherwise, the courts may lack jurisdiction to confirm the award.39 

9. Vacating, Modifying, and Correcting Awards

Section 10 of Title 9 provides for vacating awards by a U.S. 

district court for the district in which the award was made. The grounds for vacating can generally be characterized as misconduct. Section 11 gives that court authority to modify or correct errors in an award to effect its intent. Sections 12 and 13 provide the procedures for vacating, modifying, or correcting awards. 

There is, however, a threshold question of subject matter jurisdiction in each case, which is not answered by Title 9.40

The authority of a court to review an arbitrator's award is narrow. As the Supreme Court recently stated in a labor arbitration context, "a federal court may not overrule an arbitrator's

The court's powers to modify or vacate an award are limited to those specified in Title 9; however, case law suggests that an award may also be set aside "if it compels violation of law or is contrary to a well accepted and deep rooted public policy."43 Note, however, that §294(b) refers only to the grounds in Title 9 and that recent Supreme Court decisions appear to be cutting back on public policy impediments to arbitration.44

An award may be vacated pursuant to §10 (1) where the award was procured by corruption, fraud, or undue means; (2) where there was "evident partiality" or corruption of an arbitrator; (3) where there was misconduct by the arbitrators which prejudiced the rights of a party; or (4) "where the arbitrators exceeded their powers, or so imperfectly executed them that a mutual, final, and definite award upon the subject matter submitted was not made."'45 Even error in application of the law may not be a basis for vacating an award, although lack of fundamental rationality47 or manifest disregard for the law48 may provide a basis for vacating. 46

A notice of motion to modify or correct an award must also be served within three months of the filing or delivery of the award, in accordance with §12.51 The papers to be filed with such a motion, as specified in §13, must include copies of the agreement and the award. 52

The grounds for modifying or correcting an award are: (1) where there was an evident material miscalculation of figures or an evident material mistake in the description of any person, thing, or property referred to in the award; (2) where the arbitrators have awarded upon a matter not submitted to them, unless it is a matter not affecting the merits of the decision upon the matter submitted; or (3) where the award is imperfect in matter of form not affecting the merits of the controversy.53

Section 11 further provides that "[t]he order may modify and correct the award, so as to effect the intent thereof and promote justice between the parties."'54

B. Jurisdiction and Venue 

There are a number of instances in which the parties to an arbitration may have judicial recourse. The principal ones are: (1) compelling arbitration;55 .56 (2) appointing an arbitrator;5 (3) compelling attendance of witnesses;57 (4) confirming an award;58 

499 U.S.C. §§10, 12.

(5) vacating an award;59 (6) modifying or correcting an award under Title 9,60 and (7) modifying an award when patent is later found invalid or unenforceable.61

With each such application to a court there are questions of subject matter jurisdiction, personal jurisdiction, and proper venue. Because the questions involved are ones of arbitration law, the answers may not be readily apparent to patent attorneys. Because §294 is part of the Patent Act, it is possible that the answers in arbitrations involving patent validity or infringement are not the same as in other types of arbitration. 

1. Subject Matter Jurisdiction The subject matter jurisdiction of U.S. district courts is limited to subjects specified by statute.

There has been continuing pressure to abolish diversity jurisdiction of the federal courts.

Ch. 37 

General Federal Law subject matter jurisdiction on the district court. Absent such jurisdiction, however, one may have to seek the remedies specified in Title 9 and §294(c) in a state court, unless subject matter jurisdiction in the federal courts is created by §294. This could occur by either of two interpretations of §294: (1) as directly conferring subject matter jurisdiction, or (2) as making the proceeding a "civil action arising under any Act of Congress relating to patents," conferring patent jurisdiction on the district court. 67

Section 294 itself does not expressly confer subject matter jurisdiction on the federal district courts. It does not use the jurisdictional language employed where Congress has conferred subject matter jurisdiction.68 It simply states that certain arbitration matters "shall be governed by Title 9." Title 9 does not confer subject matter jurisdiction.69 The only reference in §294 to a court is to modification of awards under certain circumstances by a "court of competent jurisdiction," which is not specified as being a federal court.70

The question of whether or not court proceedings with respect to arbitration of patent validity or infringement fall within the district courts, exclusive jurisdiction over "any civil action arising under any Act of Congress relating to patents"7¹ is a complex one which may not be quickly resolved. It affects not only the jurisdiction of the district courts, which may have other jurisdiction over such arbitrations, but also controls the choice of appellate court.72 In considering these matters, two principal questions come to mind: (1) Is litigation regarding patent arbitration literally a "civil action arising under any Act of Congress relating to patents"; and (2) what was the intent of Congress?

On the one hand, it might be said that patent arbitrations are authorized by §294 of the Patent Act, which incorporates by reference Title 9. As a result, any dispute arising out of or relating to the arbitration is one arising under an Act of Congress relating to patents, and therefore the federal district courts should have exclusive jurisdiction. Furthermore, the establish- 

6728 U.S.C. §1338(a). 

68 Compare, e.g., 9 U.S.C. §202; 28 U.S.C. §1338. 

69 See nn. 66-67 and accompanying text, supra. 

7035 U.S.C. §294(c). 

7128 U.S.C. §1338(a). 

72See infra §B3.

Ch. 3

 ment of the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit with exclusive jurisdiction over appeals in civil actions arising under an Act of Congress relating to patents expresses an intent to concentrate patent disputes in the federal system culminating with that court.73

On the other hand, it might be said that the sole purpose of §294 was to dispel doubts as to the arbitrability of patent validity and infringement disputes. Even where that section refers to a court (regarding modification of awards) it refers to "any court of competent jurisdiction," without limitation to federal courts.74 This indicates a lack of congressional intent to grant exclusive subject matter jurisdiction to the federal courts. 

Finally, the court proceedings in question are likely to involve contractual issues rather than patent issues, because of the limitations on review imposed by Title 9. 

In the one Federal Circuit decision to date involving subject matter jurisdiction over an arbitration question, Rhone-Poulenc Specialites Chimiques v. SCM Corp.,75 the district court's jurisdiction was not in dispute.76 

2. Personal Jurisdiction and Venue 

Unlike subject matter jurisdiction, few novel problems of personal jurisdiction and venue are likely to arise in connection with patent arbitration. Both personal jurisdiction and venue can be consented to, and both can be waived. Thus, for example, an agreement that "judgment upon the award rendered by the Arbitrator(s) may be entered in any court having jurisdiction thereof"77 would be deemed consent to personal jurisdiction and venue in any court having subject matter jurisdiction for the purpose of confirming the award. Consent has been presumed from an agreement to arbitrate that is within a court's jurisdiction.78

General Federal Law 

Even when there has been no consent or waiver, there are fewer personal jurisdiction and venue obstacles in reaching a federal court in arbitration matters than in patent infringement litigation. The various state long-arm statutes are available to obtain personal jurisdiction." Most of them provide means for obtaining personal jurisdiction with respect to transacting any business or doing business in the state, or on any basis not inconsistent with the state and U.S. constitutions.80 

The restrictive patent venue statute applies only to "any action for patent infringement."81 The general venue statute, however, would appear to apply to civil actions involving patent arbitration issues just as it applies to declaratory judgment actions involving patent issues.82 Under that statute, venue is proper where all of the defendants reside, where "the claim arose," or where "otherwise provided by law," regardless of the basis for subject matter jurisdiction.83 Where subject matter jurisdiction is based only on diversity, venue is also proper where all of the plaintiffs reside.84

Although the courts recently have interpreted more restrictively the general venue statute's "claim arose"language, 85 a federal court having subject matter jurisdiction of an arbitration issue likely will be able to find venue as well. If the facts establishing diversity jurisdiction or where the claim arose are inadequate, support for venue might be found in the phrase "otherwise provided by law" read together with the provisions of Title 9 designating a specific federal court or courts.86 Al though federal court venue may be limited by provisions of Title 9, state court venue may not be so limited.8 87

 3. Appellate Jurisdiction 

If there is an appeal from a decision by a federal district court on a patent arbitration issue, there is a further question of which court of appeals has jurisdiction. The statutes allocating appeals between the regional courts of appeal and the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit are like a patchwork quilt: a provision here, a provision there, although all in close proximity. 88 

Section 1291 of Title 28 says that the regional courts of appeal have jurisdiction of appeals from all final decisions of the district courts except where direct review may be had in the Supreme Court. 89 The same section, however, limits the jurisdiction of the Federal Circuit to that described in §§1292(b) 

and (c) and 1295 of Title 28.9⁰ Section 1292 relates to inter locutory appeals.⁹¹ Insofar as relevant here, §1295 gives that court exclusive jurisdiction over "an appeal from a final decision of a district court * * * if the jurisdiction of that court was based, in whole or in part, on section 1338 of this title," except cases "involving a claim arising under any Act of Congress relating to copyrights or trademarks and no other claims under section 1338(a) *** 92 This is a roundabout way of indicating that the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit has jurisdiction in appeals from final decisions in civil actions which include a claim "arising under any Act of Congress relating to patents *** //93 Thus, the question of which federal appeals court would hear an appeal of a patent arbitration decision turns on the issue of whether the district court action involving a patent arbitration question was an action "arising under any Act of Congress relating to patents." 

8828 U.S.C. §§1291–1295. 

8928 U.S.C. §1291. 

90 Id. 

91 The Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit has had jurisdiction to hear interlocutory appeals of controlling questions of law certified for appeal by a district court order since the 1984 amendment of 28 U.S.C. §1292(a) and (b). Pub. L. No. 98–620, §412, 98 Stat. 3362 (1984). 

9228 U.S.C. §1295(a)(1). 

9328 U.S.C. §1338(a).

General Federal Law 

The Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit takes a fairly expansive view of its jurisdiction relating to patents.95 In Atari, Inc. v. J.S.&A. Group, Inc., the Federal Circuit held that it had jurisdiction over an appeal of a copyright decision because the original complaint had included a claim of patent infringement, in spite of the fact that the district court had ordered that the issues be tried separately.96

In Rhone-Poulenc Specialites Chimiques v. SCM Corp.,97 the complaint alleged (1) breach of contract (patent and know-how license); (2) misappropriation of trade secrets; and (3) patent infringement. The defendant moved in district court for a stay pending arbitration pursuant to the arbitration clause in the license. When the motion was denied, the defendant appealed. 

The Federal Circuit found that: "The district court has subject matter jurisdiction over the complaint based in part on 28 U.S.C. §1338(a). This court has appellate jurisdiction over the appeal under 28 U.S.C. §1295(a)(1), including pendent jurisdiction to consider the counts for breach of contract and misappropriation of trade secrets."'98

The court noted the procedural similarity to Atari in its consideration only of issues under the breach of contract count of the complaint, while its jurisdiction is founded on the patent infringement count. At least one circuit court has disagreed with 

Atari,99 so the appellate subject matter jurisdiction of an arbitration claim may depend on the court in which the appeal is filed. According to its decisions in Atari and Rhone-Poulenc, however, the Court of Appeals for the Federal Circuit will follow
