 the law of the originating regional circuit on nonpatent law issues. 100 

Another issue of federal appellate jurisdiction is whether or not the district court's decision is "final." With limited exceptions, finality is a prerequisite to appellate jurisdiction for all federal courts of appeal.101 In recent decisions, two circuit courts held that district court orders to arbitrate under §4 of Title 9 are appealable, even if the district court retains jurisdiction to resolve any further arbitrable disputes and to enforce any awards, 102

A major issue for parties seeking arbitration is whether or not an interlocutory appeal can be taken from an order which either refuses to stay litigation pending arbitration or denies a request to compel arbitration. Although the Uniform [state] 

Arbitration Act would make such orders immediately appealable, 103 the U.S. Arbitration Act is silent and the federal courts are split. In the one Federal Circuit decision in this area, Rhone- 

Poulenc, the court followed the authority of the Eleventh Circuit (in which the case originated) that "a denial of a stay to permit arbitration is appealable because it 'is an interlocutory order refusing an injunction under 28 U.S.C. §1292(a)(1)' "' and noted that in another case the Supreme Court had "accepted without comment the concept that denials of stays to permit arbitration are final and appealable decisions."'104 

Federal courts have split on the issue of appealability of an order vacating an arbitration award. The Fourth Circuit Court of Appeals found jurisdiction under the collateral order doctrine, 105 while the Ninth Circuit Court of Appeals held that it lacks such jurisdiction. 106

CHAPTER The AAA Patent Arbitration Rules* The American Arbitration Association's Patent Arbitration 

Rules, promulgated with the assistance of a special advisory committee of patent attorneys¹ in 1983, is a valuable contribution to patent dispute resolution. An agreement calling for arbitration under these rules will specify the rules as governing the arbitration and designate the AAA as administrator of the arbitration.

The patent rules are patterned after the AAA's Commercial Arbitration Rules, which have been in effect for many years. 

Like the commercial rules, the patent rules set forth standard arbitration clauses for inclusion in contracts or submission agreements; the rules governing administration and conduct of the hearing through rendition of an award, postaward proceedings; and a schedule of administrative fees. Because of the special nature of patent disputes, however, several of the patent rules differ significantly from the commercial rules. These differences are found in the rules discussing qualifications of the arbitrator (Rules 5, 13); administrative conferences (Rule 10); preliminary hearings (Rule 29); conservation of property (Rule 

34); and scope of awards (Rule 43). Each of these specialized *This chapter is a current version of Michael Hoellering's article, "The Patent Arbitration Rules of the American Arbitration Association," published in Patent Arbitration: 

What You Need to Know-A Walk Through Demonstration, and reprinted with permission of the ABA and its Litigation section. Copyright © 1985 American Bar Association. 

'Harry F. Manbeck, Jr. (Chairman), Rudolph J. Anderson, Jr., Robert B. Benson,

Jerome E. Luecke, John E. Maurer, David W. Plant, Roger S. Smith, Arthur R. Whale, and Richard C. Witte. 44

Ch. 45 

The AAA Patent Arbitration Rules rules, along with the more common arbitration rules, will be discussed in turn below.

A. Standard Arbitration Clause and Submission Agreement 

The standard arbitration clause suggested by the AAA patent rules is very similar to the standard clause found in many of the AAA rules pamphlets, including the commercial rules. 

The clause is intended for inclusion in contracts at the time of drafting, and is a provision for the private, voluntary settlement of all future disputes by arbitration. In the standard patent arbitration clause, disputes relating to patent validity or infringement are expressly specified in addition to the usual "any controversy or claim" language of broad arbitration clauses. 

Clauses with the quoted language have been held to imply that the parties intended virtually every possible contractual claim to be subject to binding arbitration.2

In those cases where there is no contractual provision for arbitration of future disputes, parties wishing to arbitrate an existing dispute may execute a submission agreement. A standard submission agreement is also suggested in the patent rules, differing from the suggested commercial submission agreement only in that it refers to the specific patent validity or infringement dispute in question, and provides for appointment of arbitrators from the patent panel. 

Patent Rule 47(c) provides that the parties are deemed to have consented to entry of judgment on the arbitration award by a state or federal court having jurisdiction. If AAA rules are not utilized it is advisable to include a provision to that effect in the arbitration agreement, if that is what the parties intend, because at least one decision has questioned whether an agreement consenting to arbitration under the AAA arbitration rules indicated a consent to entry of judgment upon an award.³ 

2See, e.g., Silverman v. Benmor Coats, Inc., 61 N.Y.2d 299, 461 N.E.2d 1261, 473 

N.Y.S.2d 774 (1984); Baker v. Sadick, 162 Cal. App. 3d 618, 208 Cal. Rptr. 676 (4th Dist. 

1984). See also Rhone-Ponlenc Specialites Chimiques v. SCM Corp., 769 F.2d 1569, 1572, 226 USPQ 873, 875 (Fed. Cir. 1985) (application of similar clause in patent license to require arbitration of scope and infringement). 

3Varley v. Tarrytown Assocs., Inc., 477 F.2d 208, 210 (2d Cir. 1973).

Ch. 4

B. Prehearing Considerations 

1. Agreement of the Parties 

By the terms of Patent Rule 1, the patent rules are incorporated by reference into the arbitration agreement of the parties to a dispute. Relying on this provision in other AAA rules, courts in several jurisdictions have held that where the rules differ from statutory provisions, the terms of the rules prevail.¹ 

2. Authority and Duties of the AAA 

Patent Rules 2 through 6 set out some of the preliminary matters to be considered by the parties, such as the designation of the appointed arbitral panel as the Patent Arbitration Tribunal (Rule 2), the authority of the AAA to act as administrator of the arbitration (Rule 3), and the authority of the AAA to delegate those administrative duties within the association (Rule 4).

Rule 5 establishes that the AAA will maintain a National Panel of Patent Arbitrators "which will include individuals having expertise in patent law and/or special technical expertise." 

This is a unique provision in that it specifies, in part, the requisite qualifications of panel members, thus assuring that parties desiring arbitration of their patent controversies will have the choice of suitable experts-patent attorneys or industry specialists. 

Rule 6, like Rule 4, establishes the right of the AAA to delegate and assign administration of arbitrations to any of its appropriate regional offices. Only Rule 3, among those listed above, corresponds to a rule which has been the subject of court decisions discussing the authority and jurisdiction of the 

AAA over a given dispute.5 

4See, e.g., Weilwood Fabrics Int'l v. Zerbi, 90 A.D.2d 453, 454 N.Y.S.2d 439 (1st Dept. 

1982); Thermasol, Ltd. v. Drieske, 78 A.D.2d 838, 433 N.Y.S.2d 166 (1st Dept. 1980), aff'd, 52 N.Y.2d 1069, 420 N.E.2d 401, 438 N.Y.S.2d 520, cert. denied, 454 U.S. 826 (1981); 

Coleman v. Southeastern Pa. Transp. Auth., 335 A.2d 413 (Pa. Super. 1975). 

5See, e.g., Forrest v. Hotel Conquistador, Inc., 193 Cal. App. 2d 503, 14 Cal. Rptr. 349 (1961); Hirsch v. duPont Glore Forgan & Co., N.Y.L.J., Feb. 5, 1974, at 16, col. 8 (Sup. 

Ct., N.Y. County).

Ch. 47 

The AAA Patent Arbitration Rules 3. Initiating Arbitration 

Rules 7-9 discuss the mechanics of beginning the actual arbitration process. They are identical to the parallel provisions in the commercial rules, except that Rule 7 of the patent rules omits its counterpart's reference to the use of expedited procedures for claims under $15,000.6

Rule 7 provides that notice of a party's intent to initiate arbitration is to be given to the other party. This notice may be referred to as a "demand" for arbitration and must set forth the initiating party's "complaint." Three copies of this notice must also be filed, along with the appropriate administrative fee, in any of the AAA's regional offices. The other party is then notified of the initiation by the AAA, and may file two copies of an "answer" with the AAA within 20 days, in which case it must also send a copy to its adversary. If no answer is filed within the stated time, it will be assumed that the claim is denied. In its answering statement a party may set forth a counterclaim, if desired, and if the counterclaim is for money damages, must also forward to the AAA an appropriate fee. 

There are a number of state court decisions arising under the commercial rules which discuss several aspects of the initiation of arbitration under Rule 7.7 Pursuant to Rule 8, a claim may be changed within 20 days of the original filing, up until the appointment of an arbitrator. After that point, however, the arbitrator(s) must approve any change of claim. 

4. Administrative Conference

Rule 10 provides for a pre-arbitration conference, either at the parties' request or in the AAA's discretion, in order to "facilitate the administrative arrangements" for the hearings. 

Such arrangements may include agreements regarding the selection of suitable arbitrators, the location of hearings, the schedule for a preliminary hearing before the arbitrator(s), pro- "Commercial Rule 7 authorizes the AAA, in its discretion, to apply the expedited procedures "in any case where the total claim of any party does not exceed $15,000, exclusive of interest and arbitration costs." The commercial rules which govern such expedited procedures also are omitted from the patent rules (Commercial Rules 5458). 

7See, e.g., Initial Trends, Inc. v. Campus Outfitters, Inc., 58 N.Y.2d 896, 447 N.E.2d 

48, 460 N.Y.S.2d 500 (1983); Thermasol, supra note 4.

Ch. 4

 visions for a stenographic record, the services of an interpreter, financial arrangements, and any other preliminary matters of an administrative, rather than substantive, nature. Although the language of the rule covering administrative conferences in the patent rules differs somewhat from the parallel provision in the commercial rules (primarily because of the expanded prehearing involvement of the arbitrators contemplated by Rule 29), its purpose is essentially the same-to make the administration of the arbitration as expeditious as possible. 

A committee of the New York Patent, Trademark and

Copyright Association, Inc., has drafted a model pretrial order for district court patent cases (see Appendix D). While not directly applicable to patent arbitrations, it may be referred to for ideas as to what might be suggested at the pre-arbitration conference, especially if the parties contemplate discovery. 

5. Fixing of Locale

The parties may choose a mutually agreeable location for the arbitration to take place. If they are unable to so agree, or fail to do so within 20 days, the AAA has the authority, under Rule 11, to designate an appropriate locale. The association, in making a locale determination, considers such factors as the location of the parties, location of witnesses and documents, relative cost to parties, place of performance of the contract, applicable law, place of previous court actions, and location of the most appropriate arbitral panel. The AAA's decision on locale matters is final and binding. Such decisions have been upheld in court.8 If only one of the parties makes a locale request, and the other fails to object within 20 days of notification, the requested locale will be granted. 

6. The Arbitrator

As noted previously, arbitrators on the AAA National Panel of Patent Arbitrators have special expertise, either in patent law 

See, e.g., Aerojet-General Corp. v. American Arbitration Ass'n, 478 F.2d 248 (9th Cir. 

1973); L.R. Foy Constr. Co. v. Dean L. Dauley & Waldorf Assocs., 547 F. Supp. 166 (D.

Ch. 49 

The AAA Patent Arbitration Rules or in some technical field. This provision is one which makes arbitration an attractive alternative to litigation. Because the panel must include such highly specialized arbitrators, the selection of appropriate experts as arbitrators is made easier. 

Except where the AAA determines that a panel of several arbitrators would be better to resolve the case, or where the parties agree to a multi-arbitrator panel, a single arbitrator hears and determines the dispute, as provided in Rule 17. The advantages of a single arbitrator are important: hearings are easier to schedule, per diem fees for the arbitrator are less expensive, and the hearings are likely to proceed more expeditiously. 

Rule 13 allows each party maximum flexibility in choosing the arbitrator, and results in a neutral, mutually agreeable selection. Based on the nature of the claim, the AAA submits an identical list of proposed arbitrators to each party. The list describes briefly the arbitrator's affiliations and expertise. Each party may then, within seven days of the mailing date of the list, strike the names of unacceptable arbitrators, number the remaining names in order of preference, and return the list to the AAA. An arbitrator is then appointed from the remaining names on both lists. If the parties are unable to agree on any acceptable arbitrator within the time specified by the rules or the parties' agreement, the AAA may make an administrative appointment. Rules 14 and 15 set out the procedure for appointment of arbitrators where the parties prefer to appoint arbitrators directly. The association notifies the arbitrator of his appointment, at which point arrangements are made for the scheduling of hearings.

Aside from the requirements of special expertise, arbitrators are expected to be neutral and impartial. Rule 12 requires neutrality except when the arbitrators are directly appointed by the parties pursuant to Rule 14. The U.S. Supreme Court ruled that the appearance of bias on the part of an arbitrator may result in vacatur of the award rendered. In addition, the U.S. 

Arbitration Act, 10 the Uniform Arbitration Act,¹¹ and virtually all other arbitration statutes provide that a party may overturn an award for evident partiality. Some courts expressed the opinion that even party-appointees must be neutral rather than 

Commonwealth Coatings Corp. v. Continental Casualty Co., 393 U.S. 145 (1968). 

109 U.S.C. §10(b) 119 U.S.C. §12(2)

Ch. 4

1 partisan;12 however, it has been recognized that in certain highly specialized fields, arbitrators may be unable to avoid nondisqualifying contacts with parties and their counsel. 13 In any event, under Rule 19, arbitrators must disclose any past and present relationships with either party which are likely to create an appearance of bias. 14 If the arbitrator is then challenged, and the parties disagree as to his removal, the AAA determines whether the arbitrator should be disqualified.15 

Rule 16 provides that, in cases where the parties are from different nations and both parties so request, the sole or neutral arbitrator may be appointed from among nationals of a country other than that of any of the parties. This differs from the commercial rules, which allow one party to make such a request. The recognition of the need for arbitrators familiar with 

U.S. patent law accounts for this variation. 

In the event that an arbitrator appointed under the rules resigns, dies, or is otherwise unable to serve, the office may be declared vacant and the matter reheard, at the discretion of the AAA, unless the parties mutually agree otherwise.16 

C. The Arbitration Hearing 

Rules 21-40 govern various aspects of the arbitration hearings. Under these provisions, the arbitrator is granted broad authority to schedule and conduct the hearings as he or she sees fit. As with other arbitration rules, however, the parties are always free to mutually agree on an alternative procedure, and their choice will prevail.

One of the most important aspects of arbitration is that it is a private forum; public attendance is not permitted, and there is no public record of the proceedings. The arbitrator may order

The AAA Patent Arbitration Rules the exclusion of everyone from the hearing except parties and their counsel, unless the parties otherwise agree." 

1. The Arbitrator's Authority 

17 The power of the arbitrator to conduct the hearings in accordance with his or her own discretion is most obvious in the rules governing scheduling of hearings, attendance, and adjournments (Rules 21, 25, 26, and 30), and those governing order of proceedings and presentation of evidence (Rules 2934). Because the arbitrator is free to shape the proceedings in these areas, they are at times challenged by a disgruntled party. 

In most cases, where the arbitrator has been fair in exercising discretion, the resulting award will not be overturned. 18 For reasons of due process, however, certain actions by the arbitrator, such as refusing to hear material evidence or refusing to adjourn a hearing for good cause, may result in vacatur of the award.19 The various arbitration statutes do not address the conduct of hearings in great detail; where they are discussed, the provisions are generally parallel to those in the AAA rules. 20 

A significant difference between arbitration and litigation centers around the presentation of evidence. The strict evidentiary rules that govern the presentation and admissibility of evidence in both the federal and state courts do not apply in arbitration. The standard generally adopted by the arbitrator for admissibility of evidence is one which admits any evidence deemed pertinent and material, and excludes evidence which is irrelevant, repetitive, or otherwise deemed unnecessary.

Within the scope of fairness and due process, the arbitra- 

17 See Johns Constr. Co. v. Unified School Dist., 233 Kan. 527, 664 P.2d 821 (1983). 

18See Harwyn Luggage, Inc. v. Henry Rosenfeld, Inc., 90 A.D.2d 747, 456 N.Y.S.2d 3 (1st Dept. 1982), aff'd, 58 N.Y.2d 1063, 449 N.E.2d 422, 462 N.Y.S.2d 642 (1983). See generally "Conduct of Hearings: The Arbitrator's Authority," AAA Lawyers' Arbitration Letter Vol. 5, No. 1 (Dec. 1981); 5 AM. JUR. 2d Arbitration §145; Annot., 75 A.L.R.3d 

132. 

19See Totem Marine Tug & Barge, Inc. v. North Am. Towing, Inc., 607 F.2d 649 (5th 

Cir. 1979). 

20 The U.S. Arbitration Act provides no guidelines for the conduct of hearings except for authorizing arbitrators to summon witnesses or other evidence (9 U.S.C. §7). The 

Uniform Arbitration Act authorizes arbitrators to schedule hearings (§5), and provides for the production of evidence (§7). Both provide for vacatur of an award where the arbitrator exceeds his or her powers or are guilty of misconduct, fails to hear material evidence or postpones a hearing for good cause, or otherwise prejudices the rights of a party. 9 U.S.C. §10(c) and (d); Uniform Act §12(3) and (4).

Ch. 4

 tor's authority with regard to admissibility of evidence is plenary, and allows him or her to fulfill the duties of managing and expediting the proceedings.21 Documentary evidence and testimony by affidavit is used in arbitration, but, in the words of Rule 32, will be given "only such weight as the Arbitrator deems it entitled to after consideration of any objections made to its admission." The arbitrator is empowered to subpoena witnesses and documents, where so authorized under the applicable law (in this case, Title 9, §7, of the U.S. Code).22 A unique feature of the patent rules specifies that the arbitrator may subpoena witnesses by "directing the subpoena to an organization which will be responsible for designating an appropriate witness."

Two other provisions are unique to the patent rules. Rule 

29 covers the procedure for a preliminary hearing, at which the arbitrator establishes a schedule for production of evidence, requests a list of witnesses, attempts to have the parties stipulate to uncontested facts and the issues to be resolved, and establishes a schedule for future hearings. This preliminary hearing is designed to make the arbitration proceedings as expeditious as possible. Because the nature of patent controversies may require a degree of discovery for resolution, Rule 29 was designed to retain the advantages of arbitration as a more expeditious forum, yet allow for the necessary degree of informational exchanges required by the dispute at hand. Thus, the rule provides that "[c]onsistent with the expedited nature of arbitration, the Arbitrator shall, at the preliminary hearing, establish (i) * *** a schedule for any hearings to elicit facts solely within the knowledge of one party *** " 

The second unique provision is found in Rule 34, which governs conservation of property. Although a similar rule may be found in virtually all other AAA rules, the patent rules' version specifically authorizes the arbitrator to take interim action as deemed necessary in order to protect trade secrets or other proprietary information which may be disclosed during the course of a patent dispute.23

23 See generally Josephs, Arbitrators' Prehearing Capacities, NAT'L L.J., Apr. 14, 1986, at 19.

The AAA Patent Arbitration Rules 2. Other Rules Governing the Hearing 

Under the AAA rules and most arbitration laws, the parties have the right to be represented by counsel at any stage of the proceeding. The rules further provide that a party may request the making of a stenographic record, at its own cost, or may have an interpreter present at the hearings. Although it has been argued in judicial proceedings that the absence of an official transcript of the arbitration hearing prejudiced a party's right to appeal,24 this is not a statutory ground for vacation of an arbitration award.

There are certain ministerial provisions among these rules. 

They provide for the taking of an oath by the arbitrator (Rule 

27), which is required by law in some jurisdictions, 25 provide the mechanisms for closing and reopening hearings26 or extending time periods (Rules 35, 36, and 39), and cover the subject of waiver (Rules 37 and 38).27 

3. The Award 

The culmination of the arbitration proceeding is the award. 

Rules 41-45 discuss different procedures surrounding the making and delivery of the award. The award must be in writing and signed by the arbitrator or, in cases where a multi-arbitrator panel has served, must be signed by a majority of the arbitrators.28 In some jurisdictions the signature of the arbitrators may be sufficient; in others, however, the award may require ac-

According to the law in some states, an arbitration award may be upset where it is untimely, resulting in prejudice to a party.31 Under the patent rules the award must be rendered within 60 days from the closing of hearings, as opposed to the 30-day limitation under the commercial rules. This extra time provides the arbitrator with the ability to fully analyze the often complex and highly technical questions which may be encountered in a patent controversy.

Perhaps the broadest grant of authority to the arbitrator under the patent rules is set forth in Rule 43, which describes the scope of the award. Here the arbitrator is given the power to grant "any remedy or relief" deemed just.32 Patent Rule 43 further provides that the arbitrator is specifically empowered to enjoin infringement, where necessary. This broad authority is further supplemented by Rule 53, granting the arbitrator the power to interpret the rules as they apply to the scope of the arbitrator's power.

The quasi-judicial immunity of the arbitrators and administering associations is protected under the U.S. Arbitration Act for actions surrounding the arbitral process.35 A party's recourse is vacatur of the award under the applicable arbitration statute, assuming the complaint is one of the grounds specified for vacatur. 36

While reliance on this provision of the patent rules should be sufficient, at least one case casts doubt on the effectiveness of a mere designation of the rules as a consent to judicial entry of judgment upon the award.37 Thus, it is better practice to specify in the arbitration agreement, if the parties so intend, that a judgment upon the award may be entered in any court having jurisdiction.38 On the other hand, if that is not the parties' intent-for example, if they intend to enter into nonbinding arbitration-they should expressly disclaim Rule 47(c) or any counterpart of that rule in the rules specified in the agreement.

Ch. 4

 E. Fees and Expenses 

The patent rules provide for the allocation of certain fees in the award, and set forth an administrative fee schedule. 

While it is generally held that attorney's fees may not be granted by the arbitrator unless specified in the parties, agreement,40 there is some authority for the granting of attorney's fees where the governing statute permits them.41

Administrative fees are determined at the time the arbitration is initiated, in accordance with the amount of damages sought. Thus, these fees often are paid in advance. The arbitrator has the authority, however, under Rule 50, to assess costs and expenses as deemed just. The AAA retains the authority to determine the per diem fee to be paid to the arbitrator where the parties are unable to agree, and may require parties to make deposits for the fees in advance of the award. 

40 See generally "Remedies in Arbitration," AAA Lawyers' Arbitration Letter, Vol. 8, No. 2 (June 1984). 

41 See Kamakazi Music Corp.v. Robbins Music Corp., 534 F. Supp. 57, 217 USPQ 51 (S.D.N.Y. 1981), aff'd, 684 F.2d 228 (2d Cir. 1982); but see Cuevas v. Potamkin Dodge, Inc., 455 So. 2d 398 (Fla. App. 1984).

CHAPTER Framing Issues for Arbitration 

The issues to be framed for arbitration critically depend on the negotiations between the parties. The parties may have great incentive to negotiate many matters in an attempt to make as few as possible, and as narrow as possible, the issues to be decided by the arbitrator. The advantages of doing this include cutting arbitration time and expense, minimizing arbitrator capriciousness, reducing the consequences of loss, and obtaining tactical advantage. Realizing any or all of these largely depends on the parties' creativity and skill in framing the issues to be arbitrated. 

Creativity in framing the issues may be exercised both when a clause to arbitrate future disputes is negotiated and later when a dispute arises requiring arbitration. Even if there is an agreement to arbitrate future disputes, when a dispute does arise one of the parties may contend that one or more of the issues in dispute are not within the scope of the arbitration clause, while the other party may contend the contrary. Such an impasse may be resolved either by the arbitrator or by a judicial determination of the arbitrability of the issues in dispute. Because of the possibility of an impasse, negotiation of future disputes clauses should be undertaken with a view to framing and delineating the issues for arbitration absent later mutual agreement.

A. Disagreement on Arbitrability of Issues 

The question of the intent of the parties to arbitrate an issue under a pre-existing arbitration clause may be brought 57

Ch. 5

 before a court by a party moving to stay a pending action until the dispute has been arbitrated,¹ or a party moving for an order to compel arbitration.2 Courts have considered a number of factors in determining the presumed intent of the parties as to the arbitrability of a dispute. 

In Wyatt Earp Enterprises, Inc. v. Sackman, Inc.,3 Wyatt Earp 

Enterprises, the producer of a TV series, licensed Sackman to use the name and likeness of Hugh O'Brian "in the characterization of Wyatt Earp." Upon expiration of the license, Sackman continued to market play clothes in boxes marked "WYATT 

EARP" and "official outfit," but deleted O'Brian's name and likeness. The agreement between the parties provided for arbitration of any dispute "arising out of or relating to" the licensing contract. Wyatt Earp moved to preliminarily enjoin 

Sackman from manufacturing and merchandising children's playsuits under the name, mark, and symbol "WYATT EARP." 

Sackman moved to stay and compel arbitration under 9 U.S.C. 

§§3 and 4. 

In refusing to compel arbitration, the court found the controversy to be one of unfair competition, to "sound not in contract but in tort": "Even if any existing subordinate contract issues were resolved by arbitration, 'the issue involved' would remain. Indeed, in the absence of a license agreement and of defendant's operation

No arbitration decision interpreting the contract could determine the controversy at bar. *** Whether or not defendant has competed unfairly with plaintiff presents an issue far transcending one merely 'arising out of or relating to the contract between the parties, and it is inconceivable that they intended such a dispute to be settled by arbitration."4

Wyatt Earp may simply hold that a licensing contract which contains an arbitration clause does not render arbitrable disputes arising between the parties, after contract termination, involving related, but not identical property. But the court does not set forth Sackman's basis for asserting that the dispute was arbitrable. There is no way of knowing whether Wyatt Earp's claim involved express or implied contractual obligations of Sackman that were to continue beyond the term of the license, or, apart from the court's conclusory statement, whether the parties intended the arbitration clause to cover the dispute. This allows Wyatt Earp to be read more broadly: Because the issue is one of tort, the contract's arbitration clause is inapplicable.

The broader reading of Wyatt Earp was not followed in Saucy Susan Products, Inc. v. Allied Old English, Inc.5 Allied sold to one Adelman certain trademarks and trade names and further granted Adelman a license to use formulae in producing products under the trademarks and names. Allied was licensed back the right to manufacture food sauces under the trademarks and names, Adelman agreeing to buy from Allied all his requirements for the food sauces for 10 years. Both the agreement embodying these terms, and an agreement hiring Allied's president as Adelman's consultant in the manufacture and promotion of the sauces, contained a clause calling for arbitration of "[a]ny controversy or claim arising out of or relating to [the] agreement or the breach thereof * * * Adelman assigned the agreements to Saucy Susan, which operated under them for almost three years. When serious differences arose, Allied commenced arbitration; seven days later, Saucy Susan sued for trademark infringement and unfair competition. Allied moved the court to stay and compel arbitration. 

Saucy Susan argued, inter alia, that the parties did not *Id. at 626-627, 116 USPQ at 125–126. (emphasis in original).

The Federal Circuit Court of Appeals held that a patent infringement case should be stayed pending arbitration on whether the item in dispute was subject to a royalty.12 The clause in the contract there was that "any controversy or claim arising out of or relating to this agreement or breach thereof shall *** be settled by arbitration ***." The court reasoned that the scope and infringement of the patent in dispute were the essence of the agreement and that the parties intended such central determinations to be included within the scope of the broad arbitration clause. 

Aside from whether an action "sounds in tort," another significant factor may be whether contractual relations between the parties remain in effect or have been terminated at the time of the dispute. In both Wyatt Earp and Homewood, the grantor had terminated the agreement and the grantee continued some use. In both cases, the grantor's subsequent action against the grantee for continued use was found not arbitrable. By contrast, 11 Id. at 1204, 179 USPQ at 702-703.

The possibility that continuing relations between the parties suggest arbitration is consistent with the belief that arbitration is less damaging to an ongoing business relationship. 

It is also consistent with Consumer Concepts, Inc. v. Mego Corp., which involved breach of a nondisclosure agreement containing no arbitration clause. The court characterized the case as presenting an "international commercial transaction" and found especially compelling arbitration of disputes having an international character: "The uncertainties arising from possible adjudication in forums far away, conducted in a foreign language, with unknown or alien procedures and substantive rules, plainly are more substantial in such a situation. *** In Scherk v. Alberto-Culver Co., [417 U.S. 506 (1974)(5-4 decision),] the Court indicated that the international character of an arbitration agreement constituted a special reason for judicial deference to the arbitral process *** "/18 The court went on to determine whether the issue fell within the scope of the arbitrator's authority, for if it did not, "[t]he argument in favor of defense to the arbitral process fails, of course ***." It found that the parties did contemplate arbitration of questions relating to the statute of limitations,19 and that there was no compelling reason not to allow an arbitrator to decide such a legal question.20

B. Framing Patent Issues 

1. Infringement and Validity

The question of infringement can be framed broadly (i.e., is any claim of patent X infringed by any of D's products?) or narrowly (i.e., is claim W of patent X infringed by D's product 

Z?). Resolving a question of infringement also involves analysis of the patent's claims and specification, its file histories, the 

17 Hanes Corp., supra note 15, 531 F.2d at 596, 189 USPQ at 339. 

18531 F.2d at 597. See also Mitsubishi Motors Corp. v. Soler Chrysler-Plymouth, Inc., 473 U.S. 614 (1985), finding arbitration of antitrust clauses appropriate where the contract was an international one. 

19531 F.2d at 598, 189 USPQ at 340–341. 

20 Id. at 599, 189 USPQ at 341–342. The opinion does not discuss the possible rele vancy of 9 U.S.C. §§3 and 4.

Ch. 5 

 th prior art, and the nature of the invention. The arbitrator can be directed to consider any or all of these particulars.21

It is not surprising to find that the arbitrability of patent validity and infringement disputes has been questioned, not on public policy grounds, but because of the difficulty in resolving these matters. 22 Specifically, because of the myriad and often complex factors typically considered in deciding infringement and validity issues, giving an arbitrator broad powers allows him great discretion in reaching a decision, with an increased potential for an arbitrary result. This potential is further aggravated by the absence of procedural safeguards taken for granted in disputes resolved by courts. First, unless the parties request one, a written opinion explaining the arbitrator's decision is not required and, indeed, is expressly discouraged by the American Arbitration Association.23 Absent a written opinion, there may be no way to know whether the arbitrator actually considered the points presented.24 Second, there is generally no substantive review of the arbitrator's decision. 

Thus, to minimize the potential for an arbitrary decision with regard to infringement and validity issues, the parties have substantial incentive to define precisely the issues for arbitration25 or, perhaps put another way, to limit the scope of the arbitrator's authority with respect to the procedures to be followed.26 

One remedy for potential arbitrariness in the decision-making process is to frame the issues in a way that forces the arbitrator to consider all the relevant points. This could be done in the context of deciding an infringement question, by presenting the arbitrator with a series of questions, akin to special interrogatories given to a jury. Thus, rather than framing the

Intel, for example, the parties agreed that the question of infringement to be submitted to the arbitrator would be restricted to deciding whether one claim of a Shell patent was infringed by either of two Intel memory products, with the selection of the claim and product to be made by Shell after review of Intel circuit diagrams. 

The parties further agreed that the arbitrator could consider the relevant prior art and the patent's file history for the purpose of determining claim scope.

23R. COULSON, BUSINESS ARBITRATION-WHAT YOU NEED TO KNOW 25 (2d ed. 1982) 

("Written opinions can be dangerous because they identify targets for the losing party to attack.").

26 See id. at 116.

Ch. 65 

Framing Issues for Arbitration infringement issue as "Is claim 1 infringed?" the arbitrator can be asked to decide a series of written questions. As a simple example, assume the claim in issue reads as follows:

A product comprising a cone with an epoxy coating thereon. 

The infringement issue can be framed as follows: (1) (a) Does product X have a cone? (b) If so, does product X have an epoxy coating on the cone? (2) If the answer to (1)(a) or (1)(b) is no, does product X perform substantially the same function as a cone with an epoxy coating thereon, and does it do so in substantially the same way to obtain the same result?

As can be seen, question 1 asks factual questions directed to whether or not the claim is literally infringed. Question 2 asks factual questions directed to whether or not the claim is infringed under the doctrine of equivalents.27 The arbitrator need not be asked to decide the ultimate question of infringement; presumably, the parties can agree that liability arises if either question is answered yes.

Such questions force the arbitrator to focus on specified issues. As in question 2 above, they can also incorporate what the parties agree to be the applicable law; this may make it more palatable to use someone other than a patent law expert as an arbitrator. One aspect of framing issues in detail is that as the level of detail in the questions increases, the answers the arbitrator gives start to resemble in substance a written opinion. 

Yet another alternative is to decide infringement by way of a fictitious claim. Again, this can be used to reduce arbitrator discretion. Specifically, a fictitious claim can be used to eliminate application of the doctrine of equivalents by the arbitrator, along with its attendant discretion. In the same vein, rather than having an arbitrator decide what a "means" claim element is, the parties can negotiate a specific meaning for purposes of arbitration and substitute that meaning in the claim. Use of a fictitious claim transforms the dispute from one of patent infringement to one of contract.

Apart from noninfringement, invalidity is the other principal defense of an accused infringer. Here again, the parties can negotiate the scope of the invalidity issues to be addressed, 27 See Graver Tank & Mfg. Co. v. Linde Air Prod. Co., 339 U.S. 605, 608, 85 USPQ 328, 330 (1950); Hughes Aircraft Co. v. United States, 717 F.2d 1351, 1364, 219 USPQ 473, 481 (Fed. Cir. 1983).

Ch. 5 

HU ந்த if at all, by the arbitrator.28 The invalidity issues that may be decided by the arbitrator, explicitly or implicitly, or negotiated away by the parties run the gamut, for example, the level of ordinary skill in the art, the scope and content of the art, the "secondary" considerations, which may or may not now be secondary.29 The parties may also agree to limit the prior art in question to specific patents, with the obviousness question determined by the arbitrator in light of those patents. 

If a fictitious claim is used, or if any part of an actual claim is modified, some consideration will need be given to whether invalidity is being asserted against the fictitious claim or an actual patent claim. If a finding of invalidity in arbitration will not collaterally estop the patentee from future litigation,30 then an assertion of invalidity against a claim other than the claim in arbitration, fictitious or real, makes little sense. On the other hand, arbitration with respect to a modified or wholly fictitious claim may minimize the chances that a court in future litigation will find collateral estoppel applicable, where the arbitrator found the arbitrated "claim" invalid. 

With regard to issues of inadequate disclosure and enablement under 35 U.S.C. §112, the patentee may prefer to have, as arbitrator, a technical expert, rather than a patent lawyer, if 28 In Shell v. Framing Issues for Arbitration the arbitrator is to decide whether there is an enabling disclosure. If the issue is best mode, the patentee may want to incorporate a scienter requirement into the question presented to the arbitrator, i.e., whether or not the best mode was intentionally withheld. The patentee may alternatively or additionally desire that the issue be whether the best mode was disclosed at all. The accused infringer, in contrast, may want the issue to be whether the best mode was adequately disclosed. The patentee can circumvent an issue of whether or not a claim is indefinite by proposing a fictitious claim. 

2. Damages or Royalties Due 

The question of damages or royalties due may be the most important of all. Indeed, in some circumstances the parties may prefer not to submit the money issues to arbitration, but rather may agree on the level of damages or royalties owed in the event of the arbitrator's findings resulting in liability.³¹ Even if damages or royalties are to be decided by the arbitrator, that issue can be reserved until the question of liability is decided, thus bifurcating the arbitration. 

3. Interferences 

Settlement of priority of invention disputes via arbitration had an early and auspicious start. Section 9 of the Patent Act of 179332 provided that cases involving interfering applications were to be submitted to three arbitrators, with the majority decision of the arbitrators being final with respect to the grant- 3¹In Shell v. Intel, a license was executed which provided that a contingent payment of about $500,000 would be made by Intel if the arbitrator's determination of infringement was favorable to Shell. See Borovoy & Janicke, supra note 28, at 260–261. See also Robb, The Arbitration of Patent Controversies, 25 J. PAT. OFF. SOC'Y 412, 414-415 

(1943), which describes an arbitration agreement that took the form of a patent license, with the amount of royalties provided for in advance, but conditioned upon the validity and scope of patents as determined by the arbitrators. 

32 Laws of February 21, 1793, ch. 11, 1 Stat. 318–323, repealed by Patent Act of 1836, ch. 57, §21, 5 Stat. 117.

Ch. 5

 ing of a patent.33 Recently passed 35 U.S.C. §135(d) again makes it clear that interferences can be arbitrated. 

4. Interpretation of License Provisions 

In contrast to validity and infringement questions, only recently given legislative approval, many questions interpreting license provisions are traditionally dealt with in commercial arbitration.34 Issues of license interpretation that have been decided include whether a front payment could be applied against royalties owed,35 and whether a license provision's reference to "dollars" meant U.S. currency or a combination of U.S. and foreign currency.36 Questions such as these should be relatively straightforward to frame for arbitration. 

However, many questions of license provision interpretation may be as complex, and perhaps more complex, than those of infringement or validity; e.g., is X a licensed product under an agreement licensing X's manufacturer under 10 different patents for specific fields of use? When presented with such questions, the parties may have substantial incentive to frame the issues as a series of simple questions. 

C. Arbitration of Other Intellectual Property Issues 

Generally, arbitration of other intellectual property disputes, such as trademark, copyright, and trade secret disputes, is outside the scope of this book. Such disputes have always been susceptible to handling by arbitration, not being invested with the public policy problems of patent disputes. Since a patent dispute often is associated with another type of intellectual property dispute and because patent practitioners often 

33 One interference arbitration under this statute involved Robert Fulton and John

L. Sullivan, apparently rival claimants to a combination of several boats connected so as to be propelled by one steam-driven boat. Fulton reportedly chose Eli Whitney as an arbitrator. Federico, Early Interferences and the Case of Robert Fulton vs. John L. Sullivan, 19 J. PAT. OFF. Soc'Y 761, 764-765 (1937). 

34See Davis, Patent Arbitration, 15 ARB. J. 127, 132 (1960).

Ch. 69 

Framing Issues for Arbitration handle other intellectual property matters as well, some mention is here made about arbitration of such disputes. 

First, such disputes can be handled in the mainstream of arbitration. Because §294 of Title 35 of the U.S. Code is limited to the special problems of patent disputes, all of the rules, procedures, and considerations generally applicable to arbitration of disputes are pertinent here.37 For example, arbitrations can deal with any and all aspects of trademarks. Thus, they can deal with the issue of infringement of trademarks registered under the laws of any country, including the laws of the United States, or infringement of unregistered-i.e., common lawtrademarks. Also, oppositions, interferences, or cancellations involving trademarks may be decided by arbitration.

Secondly, the considerations detailed previously with respect to framing the issues in patent disputes apply as well to these disputes. Indeed, many of the cases discussed there are trademark and unfair competition cases. The parties should analyze just what issues are to be arbitrated and how best to reach the desired resolution of the dispute. As with patents, the creativity and skill of the persons framing the issues are extremely important.

Some of the more important issues to be addressed in trademark disputes are: how the issue of use or priority of use is to be addressed (by affidavit, etc.); what standard of confusing similarity the arbitrator should apply since this varies throughout the United States and the world; the question of damages, which may require considerable research as to the standards of compensation in a particular branch of industry or commerce and, in particular, may involve the testimony of accounting experts determining the standards to be applied and calculating the amounts due; and how to assess whether the alleged trademark is unique, capable of being a trademark, or simply a term of the art or a descriptive term used in a particular industry. 

As with patents, the question of who should be the arbitrator is crucial. There certainly is room for argument as to whether one or even all of the arbitrators should be lawyers, patent lawyers, trademark or copyright lawyers, or, possibly, one particularly qualified in the special technology or business the proceeding is to cover. Some parties and some advocates

 prefer the panel not to be too legalistic. When the parties select an arbitrator who has a particular expertise in a selected business-e.g., textiles, pharmaceuticals, greeting cards—there is always the danger that the expert arbitrator has a personal theory on a crucial point, which may unduly affect the outcome. ‚9,………………

CHAPTER The Arbitrator A. Selection of the Arbitrator 

The selection of the arbitrator should begin with drafting the arbitration clause. If the clause simply provides for the settlement of disputes by arbitration, one must depend upon a future agreement of the parties or, failing that, resort to the U.S. Arbitration Act¹ and have an arbitrator appointed by the court. When parties have the opportunity to establish the mechanics for settling disputes, including the most important element-a say in who shall decide the case-they are seriously remiss if they do not establish the procedure for selecting an arbitrator. 

There are at least six methods for choosing the arbitrator. 

They are: (1) The "bird in the hand" method, whereby the parties agree upon a single arbitrator and name him and perhaps an alternate in the contract. (2) The "good faith" method, whereby the parties provide for naming a single arbitrator by mutual agreement when and if disputes or grievances arise. (3) The "pass the buck" method, whereby the parties leave to an outside agency or an individual the naming of the impartial arbitrator. (4) The "log jam" method, whereby the parties provide that each will name one arbitrator (or more) and leave the selection of 

143 Stat. 883 (1924), later codified 61 Stat. 669, Title 9, U.S. Code (1947). 

it 10 71

Ch. 6

101 the impartial arbitrator to the agreement of the two so chosen or the mutual choice of the parties. (5) The "still born" method, whereby the parties agree upon a board composed of an equal number of arbitrators appointed by each party. (6) The "mutual assistance" method, whereby the parties agree to have a single impartial arbitrator, appointed under the rules of an agency maintaining a panel of arbitrators, such as the American Arbitration Association (AAA). (7) The "last man up" method, whereby the parties provide for the submission of a list of several names. For a single arbitrator selection, the list could be seven names. Each party vetoes less than half the number on the list, presumably three, thus leaving at least one eligible arbitrator.2 

When considering any of these methods, the basic question to keep in mind is: Will it produce an arbitrator(s) (1) that is impartial and competent? (2) that represents a high degree of mutual choice? (3) that can arrive at a fair decision without compromise? (4) that can be appointed in spite of any neglect or refusal on the part of a recalcitrant party to the contract?

If the parties use the AAA's Standard Patent Arbitration Clause which references the Patent Arbitration Rules, an election is thereby made of a most useful and efficient method that has been successfully used in thousands of cases and meets the principal criteria. The parties can modify the AAA procedure in drafting the arbitration clause, but the effect of such modifications on obtaining an arbitrator(s) who meets the basic criteria may be more difficult. More importantly, if the AAA procedure is used, one knows that it has been tested in the courts and thus the limited grounds for upsetting an award are minimized.

The AAA procedures are not cast in stone and in fact have considerable flexibility, even more so when the parties jointly submit proposals. For example, in a recent case, counsel agreed 

2 AMERICAN ARBITRATION ASSOCIATION, HOW TO SELECT AN ARBITRATOR: "BLIND MAN'S BLUFF"-OR AN OPEN-EYED CHOICE? (1946); Fitzpatrick, Advice on Selecting Arbitrators, 

15 LRR 433 (1944). For an interesting paper presented to the Seminar on International

Commercial Arbitration, March 15, 1967, by Shri S. Subramanian, including arbitrator selection, see Arbitrators and Their Appointment, 33 J. INST. ARBS. 168 (1967). For specimen letters of procedures in selecting arbitrators when there is no administrative body, see 

INSTITUTE OF ARBITRATORS, SPECIMEN LETTERS OF PROCEDURE IN ARBITRATION (1972).

The Arbitrator and requested that the AAA propose a three-party panel consisting of (1) an engineer with extensive hands-on experience in electronics and computers; (2) an engineer with extensive arbitration experience and experience in large-building construction; and (3) an attorney with strong construction and arbitration experience. The case involved a claim between the building owner and the subcontractor who installed the life safety and energy optimization system in a casino hotel. The complexity of the case can be judged by the fact that it required about 25 hearings.

In another case involving an international dispute between an American bank and a foreign bank, the parties requested a panel to be made up of (1) a senior executive of a bank involved in international and foreign trade; (2) another similarly qualified executive who was also a lawyer; and (3) a New York lawyer with substantial experience in international and foreign disputes. The AAA administrator, to whom the case was assigned, provided such a panel.

Even though there are no agreed-upon qualifications for arbitrators, the AAA administrator first notes the monetary amount involved in the case (Rule 17).³ In most cases of any size, a panel of three is proposed and while a single arbitrator can be appointed in a patent case (and is if the parties have agreed), the usual number in patent cases is three.

The administrator then must look to the subject matter of the dispute, i.e., the "art." This presumably can be determined from the complaint or from the title of the patent involvedeven possibly the reference to the infringing device. Better still, counsel for the parties should promptly get in touch with the administrator or the regional director for a discussion of the qualifications for the arbitrator. One can even have formal recourse to the Administrative Conference under Rule 10.

Somewhere in the early proceedings, the administrator will search the National Panel of Patent Arbitrators (Rule 5). All of the members of this panel are certified as "having experience in patent law and/or special technical expertise" and theirbackground and experience are briefly shown on a panel card. Cur- 

3 AMERICAN ARBITRATION ASSOCIATION, PATENT ARBITRATION RULES (1985), included herein as App. B. All references to “Rules” in the text are to these rules unless otherwise stated. Ch. 6

 rently the New York office and some of the regional offices have their panel lists programmed in computers from which can be secured printouts.

From the computer or the panel cards the administrator selects those persons who best meet the qualifications required for the subject of the controversy. Computer printouts or photocopies of panel cards are made and sent to each party. If a three-member panel is to be established, the list probably contains at least 15 names, which, along with the information on each, is sent to all parties with instructions to cross off those not acceptable and to leave three names, numbered in order of acceptability (Rule 13). When these lists are returned, the administrator selects the three most acceptable arbitrators and invites their acceptance to serve.

In some cases the parties return the lists with all names crossed off. It was formerly the practice to submit additional lists but this is no longer the case, and the administrator instead reviews the panel data on nonsubmitted arbitrators and selects the necessary number for the panel without recourse to those whose names were crossed off. This is called an administrative appointment, which may be for one or all members of the panel. 

The administrator then phones and invites the arbitrator to serve in the case, stating that there is a case needing arbitration, providing the names of the parties and counsel involved, and briefly describing the controversy. The administrator may also advise that the parties want the hearings expedited or give other information which will assist the arbitrator in determining his or her ability and availability to serve. 

This all brings into focus Rule 19, Disclosure and Challenge Procedure, which requires a neutral arbitrator to disclose to the AAA any circumstances likely to affect impartiality, including any bias, any financial or personal interest in the result of the arbitration, or any past or present relationships with the parties or their counsel. The requirement for disclosure is emphasized

Ch. 75 

The Arbitrator by its inclusion in the written notice of appointment, which notes that relationships which arise during the course of the arbitration must be disclosed. The arbitrator's oath is executed on the same sheet.

Because patent arbitration often involves rights under one or more patents, it is possible that another disclosure might be required. Most of the National Panel of Patent Arbitrators is made up of patent attorneys, with a few individuals having special technical expertise and probably also being involved in patent rights. The question then arises: Does the arbitrator have any present or possible future interest in the validity or scope of the patent or patents involved? It might be put another way: 

Is the arbitration to which the patent relates one in which that patent could affect any interest of the arbitrator directly or indirectly or because of an interest of a client? These and possibly other concerns may arise because a patent is the core of a dispute.

If an arbitrator finds no disqualifying factors and is appointed, he sometimes comes to an early hearing and finds a cocounsel or an important witness he might know, or some other factor that might require a disclosure. For example, after

 a number of hearings in a recent case, an arbitrator disqualified himself because the company by which he was employed had merged with a subsidiary of one of the parties. 

Usually these later developments are very minor or are waived by the parties. The practice is always to make the disclosure. Then, as in the case of pre-appointment disclosure, the parties may express their positions and, as provided in the rules, the AAA determines whether the arbitrator should be disqualified.

At the time when the choice of an arbitrator is under consideration, the parties need not rely solely on the information supplied on the AAA panel card. While there is no provision for practicing a voir dire procedure on a prospective arbitrator, one is not prevented from using acceptable methods of investigating. All members of the bar have reputations which can be discovered. In addition to the legal directory Martindale- 

Hubbell, there are many other helpful directories. Even the use of Who's Who in America and Who's Who American Law can add to the information for consideration. Since arbitration is a private proceeding and in most cases the award is a relatively simple statement, one cannot learn from prior arbitrations about the arbitrator's approach or reasoning. (There is an exception in the case of labor arbitration and the statutory arbitration provided where there will be findings of fact and conclusions of law.)

Where there is a choice, should one opt for a single arbitrator or a panel? Obviously a panel of several arbitrators will increase the costs and the difficulty in scheduling hearings. 

Based on the importance of the award and the amount of money involved, these considerations might not have great weight. 

Studies of 6and 12-person juries indicate that the larger the number the greater the likelihood of a dissenter.5 Applying this to arbitration panels, it probably will take more time to agree on an award. Also the larger the panel, the less likely there will be unanimity, thus some compromise is probable and extreme positions are minimized. The most practical and effective body to settle patent disputes is a panel of three persons carefully selected from a list prepared according to the specifications of the parties. 

5J. Kalven & P. Zeisel, THE AMERICAN JURY (1976), cited in Roth, Choosing an Arbitration Panel, 6 LITIGATION 13, 14 (Winter 1980).

Ch. 77 

The Arbitrator 

When one has considered all aspects involved in the selection of the arbitrator or panel of arbitrators, keep in mind 

Roth's conclusion: "There is no ideal arbitration panel for all disputes. Indeed, the parties to a given arbitration may have different concepts of the type of panel that should be selected. What is clear is that the composition of the arbitration panel is probably more important than the identity of the judge or jury that resolves disputes in formal trials. The lawyer who does not carefully analyze the number of arbitrators, how they were or will be selected and the nature, past experience, present position and other personal characteristics of each arbitrator, and who does not press for a panel most favorable to his client's interests and rights, is not performing his role as an advocate."6

B. Scope of the Arbitrator's Authority 

Though it can be stated in many different ways, the simplest and most accurate is: "The power of an arbitrator is awesome."7

One naturally compares this decision maker to a trial court judge. There are many similarites, but there are also significant differences. One of the most important differences is that the judge's actions in admitting or refusing evidence, the conduct of the trial, and, most important, the final decision are subject to review by an appellate tribunal. The award of the arbitrator is final and subject to the very limited reasons for vacating or modifying an award given in §10 of the U.S. Arbitration Act.8 

The award becomes a judgment, docketed as if it were rendered in an action. "The judgment so entered shall have the same force and effect, in all respects, as, and be subject to all provisions of law relating to, a judgment in action; and it may be enforced as if it had been rendered in an action in the court in which it is entered."9

The arbitrator determines whether a new claim may be filed or a claim amended (Rule 8), fixes the time and place for each hearing (Rule 21), and maintains the privacy of the hearings, "Roth, supra note 5. 

?Coulson, Arbitration in the Eighties: How to Make It Work for You, 17 FORUM 673 (1980). 

89 U.S.C. §10 (1970). 

99 U.S.C. §13.

Ch. 6

1001 in which persons may be excluded, except for a party and counsel (Rule 25). The arbitration can take adjournments (Rule 

26), and can also require witnesses to be sworn (Rule 27). Under

Rule 28 a majority of arbitrators (two of the three) can make all decisions.

The order of proceedings is determined by the arbitratorpreferably at a preliminary hearing-and a schedule is set for hearings to elicit facts solely within the knowledge of one party (Rule 29). After due notice the arbitrator may proceed in the absence of one party and, upon proofs, make an award against the defaulting party (Rule 30).

The arbitrator may issue subpoenas to a specific person or to an organization to designate a witness familiar with the described subject matter, and then rule upon the pertinence and materiality of evidence produced (Rule 31). Evidence submitted by affidavit may be considered (Rule 32). If the arbitrator deems it necessary, an inspection or investigation of a place or a device can be made (Rule 33). Rule 34 confirms the authority of the arbitrator to issue orders or interim awards to safeguard property, preserve evidence, and protect trade secrets or other proprietary information that might be disclosed during the arbitration. The arbitrator may award any just and equitable remedy or relief, including specific performance, injunctive relief, and assessment of fees and expenses (Rule 43).

In judicial proceedings relating to the subject matter of the arbitration, the arbitrator is not a necessary party and is rarely involved. The arbitrator enjoys judicial immunity, including immunity from civil liability, 10 Under Rule 53, the arbitrator "shall interpret and apply [these] rules insofar as they relate to the Arbitrator's powers and duties." Approved and recommended by both organizations, the code states that "an arbitrator has a responsibility not only to the parties, but also to the process of arbitration itself, and must observe high standards of conduct so that the integrity and fairness of the process will be preserved."

C. Compensation of the Arbitrator 

Arbitraton involves the usual costs of any litigation, although some are unique to this form of dispute settlement. The administrative fee paid to the AAA (or other administrative body that handles the case) is determined by a schedule printed in the rules and reflects the amount of the filed or amended claim and counterclaim, if any. It starts at 3 percent of the first $20,000 and decreases in increments to 1/4 percent of amounts over $160,000. When no amount is stated in a claim, a fee of $500 is applied, subject to adjustment when the amount or the type of nonmonetary relief requested can be determined. The cost of a stenographic record and any transcripts is paid by the party ordering the service, and when transcripts are prepared, copies are furnished to each arbitrator. 

Rule 50 provides that all traveling and other expenses of the arbitrator and AAA representatives, the expenses of witnesses, the cost of any proofs produced at the direct request of the arbitrator, and the neutral arbitrator's fee shall be borne equally by the parties unless they agree otherwise, or unless the arbitrator, in the award, assesses such expenses or any part thereof against any specified party or parties. 

The most significant and probably the largest expense will be the arbitrator's fee, the subject of Rule 51. While the Commercial Arbitration Rules provide that AAA panel members serve without fee except in prolonged or special cases, a joint committee of AAA staff and patent attorneys which developed the Patent Arbitration Rules believed that the members of the panel should be compensated based on a per diem (or hourly rate), taking into consideration professional rates.

The arrangements for the fee are not made directly between the parties and the arbitrator, but only by the AAA acting as

Ch. 6

 the intermediary. In case agreement cannot be secured, the 

AAA has the sole power to determine the per diem fee. This method of handling this very delicate matter is consistent with the purpose of so many of the arbitration rules, which is to insulate a neutral arbitrator from all parties and counsel, except at hearings where all parties have an opportunity to be present. 

An arbitrator's services begin even before there are any formal hearings, i.e., motions, subpoenas, or other matters, including consideration of the claim and answer and preparation for the preliminary hearing. Arbitrators can charge for their time for hearings and for study time which will depend upon whether the hearings proceed rapidly, or whether there are long intervals between sessions. After the close of the hearings and final arguments or briefs, the arbitrators also require individual study time, then joint time to agree among themselves on an award. Thus, in assessing the total costs of an arbitration, it can be assumed that each arbitrator will charge approximately the same as counsel from the time that the case gets underway. 

Parties are required to deposit sums with the AAA to keep approximately current with the accumulation of the arbitrators' fees and expenses (Rule 52).

While one of the reasons for using arbitration is its lower cost, this may not be true for the hearing stage in complicated cases with large sums at stake. One way of minimizing costs is for the arbitrator to follow some of the recommendations of Allen Poppleton on Expediting the Large and Complex Commercial Case.12 

12 Poppleton, The Arbitrator's Role in Expediting the Large and Complex Commercial Case, 36 ARB. J. 6, 10 (Dec. 1981) (reprints available from the AAA).

CHAPTER Arbitration Hearings A. Preliminary Hearings and Associated Activities 

The question of how to best conduct the hearings of a patent arbitration, and particularly the ground rules which would act as a substitute for the discovery period in conventional patent litigation provided a great challenge to the special advisory committee of patent attorneys who helped draft the 

American Arbitration Association (AAA) Patent Arbitration 

Rules. It was recognized that there should be some opportunity for each party to have access to the relevant information of the other party prior to the final arbitration hearing. Yet the committee did not want to create a discovery period which would simply transfer the high cost and burden of extensive discovery characteristic of patent litigation from the courts to the arbitration process. This would undermine several of the most significant benefits contemplated for use of the arbitration, namely, reduced cost and expedited consideration.

Simply allowing each party to present its own case at the arbitration hearing would not provide access to significant information which might be solely in the adversary party's control. By way of example, an infringing party might wish to establish invalidity based on information in the patentee's files showing public use. Or the infringing party might want to question the background of data used to establish patentability as found in the patent specification or introduced by affidavit during prosecution of the patent application. Conversely, the patent holder would certainly wish to have some prior infor- 101 IN 19 81

Ch. 7

[" mation as to the public domain publications or other grounds for invalidity to be urged by the infringing party at the arbitration hearing.

The patent advisory group was able to profit from the AAA's experience in large and complex commercial cases wherein the advantages of the arbitrator taking an active role in managing the case and setting ground rules had already been established. 

Prior exchange of information had been found to be quite helpful in expediting the hearings in such cases. For example, the simple step of providing in advance the background of a proposed expert witness and a brief outline of his opinion, as well as reports to be put in evidence, was found to be quite helpful in expediting the actual testimony of the expert at the hearing. 

In a significant departure from prior arbitration rules the study group provided for a preliminary hearing procedure as set forth in Rule 29. It has no counterpart in the AAA Rules of 

Commercial Arbitration. The key portion of Rule 29 reads as follows: 

"Consistent with the expedited nature of arbitration, the Arbitrator shall, at the preliminary hearing, establish (i) the extent of and a schedule for the production of relevant documents and other information, the identification of any witnesses to be called and a schedule for any hearings to elicit facts solely within the knowledge of one party, and (ii) a schedule for further hearings to resolve the dispute.' This portion of Rule 29 is intended to give the arbitrator a great deal of freedom in establishing ground rules for the production of relevant documents and the method by which one party is to be given an opportunity to elicit facts solely within the knowledge of the other party in advance of the principal hearing. Rule 31, following a procedure similar to Federal Rule of Civil Procedure 30(b)(6), provides that the arbitrator may subpoena witnesses by directing the subpoena to an organization and requiring the production of a witness knowledgeable about a particular area.

These powers are introduced by the phrase "[c]onsistent with the expedited nature of arbitration," which is intended to emphasize that the prehearing procedures are not to resemble the onerous discovery period characteristic of conventional patent litigation. Nevertheless Rule 29 recognizes that each party must have a degree of access to information in the other party's

Ch. 83 

Arbitration Hearings possession. How far the arbitrator should go in order to provide this opportunity remains to be tested. The arbitrator must balance the nature and extent of justified "discovery'' in the preliminary phases of arbitration as opposed to fishing expeditions. 

The process must be managed to avoid undue burdens of time and cost as well. Certainly, the judgment required in deciding such issues emphasizes the importance of choosing qualified arbitrators who are sufficiently knowledgeable in patent litigation.

The questioning of witnesses in this preliminary phase differs from conventional discovery in that it may only be carried out in the presence of the arbitrator (the "trier of the facts") as well as the opposing party (Rule 29). The arbitrator plays an active role in the questioning, such as by limiting it to relevant issues, and personally reaches conclusions as to the demeanor of witnesses and any other factors affecting the weight of the evidence introduced.

At the preliminary hearing the arbitrator also seeks to specify the issues to be resolved and works with the parties to stipulate uncontested facts. A schedule of the various steps preceding the hearing must be developed. 

In sum, Rule 29 gives the arbitrator broad powers and discretion to provide for the necessary mutual access of information and the means for soliciting evidence without incurring the abuses of discovery in litigation. Indeed this was a key reason for seeking to apply arbitration to patent disputes. 

The following is a checklist for a preliminary hearing under Rule 29 (Compare this checklist with App. D, a model pretrial order for suggested topics of discussion at a preliminary hearing in a district court proceeding.): (1) A definition of the issues to be resolved. (2) A brief written statement (not to exceed one or two pages) of each party's position as to the subject matter of the arbitration. (3) A stipulation as to uncontested facts. (4) The extent of, and schedule for, the production of relevant documents and other information. (5) The mechanism to solicit facts solely within the knowledge of each party and a schedule for doing so. (6) A list of proposed witnesses, their biographies, and an outline of their proposed testimony; arrangements for supply- 10

Ch. 7

 ing copies of any expert reports to be submitted; and a schedule for hearing such witnesses. (7) Advance filing and pre-identification of exhibits, including a schedule for them. (8) A schedule for the hearing, including estimated length of the hearing and presentation times for each party. (9) Procedures for the conduct of hearings, including the nature of briefs and due dates. (10) In recognition of the importance of the protection of trade secrets and other proprietary information, a definition of procedures to protect proprietary disclosures made during the arbitration (see Rule 34).

There normally is a great deal of reluctance to give a competitor access to technical or commercial information. Questions may arise as to whether access to such information is to be limited to nonemployees of the opposing party, e.g., outside attorneys and expert witnesses, or whether inside counsel as well as other employees (technical personnel) are to be given the opportunity to consider such information during the arbitration process. Once again the arbitrator is given broad in terms of setting ground rules as to how to proceed. 

B. Final Hearing powers The conduct of the hearing in a patent arbitration should be much the same as that of a commercial arbitration hearing with the exception that the preliminary hearing stages should have crystalized complex issues and therefore expedite the final hearing. It is obviously essential that the parties carefully prepare their cases in advance of the hearing and in a manner consistent with the prehearing activities. 

Experience has shown that complex arbitration cases can be advanced more effectively by the use of consecutive hearing dates rather than a series of sessions. This should be considered in the scheduling process; i.e., an effort should be made at the preliminary hearing to block out an uninterrupted period for the final hearing. The tribunal administration manages the arrangements for the hearing.

Throughout both the hearing and the preliminary hearing stages, the patent rules seek to expedite the arbitration process by defining the evidence to be heard as "such evidence as is pertinent and material to the controversy" (Rule 31). This con-

Ch. 85 

Arbitration Hearings trasts with the phrase "such evidence as they [the parties] desire" contained in the commercial rules. Patent arbitration is similar to commercial arbitration, however, in that “all evidence shall be taken in the presence of all the Arbitrators and of all the parties, except where any of the parties are absent, in default or has waived the right to be present" (Rule 31).

Arbitration hearings are by nature informal. The arbitrator will want to hear all the evidence that is relevant and material and in general may be quite liberal in accepting evidence since the arbitrator is not limited to the rules of evidence (Rule 31). 

There is no burden of proof in a patent arbitration; each party must prove his case to the arbitrator. Objections may be made to the evidence as being hearsay or otherwise improper. However, the arbitrator has broad power in the conduct of the hearing and the weight to be placed on the evidence submitted. 

As in the case of commercial arbitration, evidence may be taken by affidavit. However, it is given only such weight as the arbitrator deems it proper (Rule 32). In view of the opportunity for examination during the preliminary hearing stages of a patent arbitration, evidence merely by affidavit may be less acceptable in patent cases.

Documentary evidence should be presented to the arbitrator and a copy given to the other side with the key elements thereof highlighted to focus attention. Direct testimony may be the most persuasive form of evidence insofar as it relates to nonpublic domain type information. Every witness is subject to cross-examination. Each party is given a full opportunity to present its proofs and evidence, and it is only upon each party's concurrence that it has no further proofs to submit that the arbitrator shall close the hearings (Rule 35). 

It is for the arbitrator and the parties to decide if written briefs are to be submitted before the arbitrator's decision. If briefs are to be filed, the hearings are declared closed as of the final date set for receipt of the briefs.

CHAPTER The Arbitrator's Award A. Time and Form of the Award 

Under the Patent Arbitration Rules promulgated by the American Arbitration Association (AAA) the arbitrator is required to close the arbitration hearings¹ upon inquiring of all parties whether they have any further evidence to offer or witnesses to be heard.2 If briefs, affidavits, or other documents are to be filed, the hearings are declared closed as of the final date set by the arbitrator for the receipt of briefs or the receipt of other documents, whichever is later.3 The close of the hearings marks the point beyond which the parties may no longer be heard on the merits of the case. The close of hearings is also the point at which the time period within which the arbitrator must render a decision begins to run.4

Rule 41 of the patent rules provides that the award shall be made promptly by the arbitrator, no later than 60 days from the closing date of the hearings, unless otherwise agreed by the parties or specified by law.5 If oral hearings are waived, the

However, a party waives the objection that an award was not timely made unless the arbitrator is notified, in writing, of the objection prior to the delivery of the award. N.Y. CIV. PRAC.
