Whenever there is more than one arbitrator, all decisions of the arbitrators must be made by at least a majority. However, the underlying arbitration agreement or the law may require unanimity.?

Under the patent rules, the arbitrator's award is required to be in writing and signed by the arbitrator. If there is more than one arbitrator, only a majority of them need to sign the award. Rule 42 also provides that the award must be executed in the manner required by law.⁹ 

The AAA is required to deliver the award to the parties. 

Rule 45 provides that such delivery may be accomplished by mailing10 the award to the parties or their attorneys or by filing the award in any manner that may be prescribed by law. 

Although the award itself must be in writing, an arbitrator is not required to write an opinion which explains the basis for the decision. In fact, an award should consist of nothing more than a short and concise directive to the parties. An award that incorporates an opinion leaves room for court challenges which serve to delay enforcement of the award, thereby frustrating one of the advantages of arbitration (i.e., quick resolution of controversies without judicial intervention).¹¹

Similarly, an award should be clear and definite. An award ambiguous on its face can give rise to judicial intervention. In the case of an indefinite award the courts may remand it to the "Rule 36. Courts refrain from the latter practice since the Supreme Court made it clear that there is no requirement that arbitrators explain the reasons for their awards.13 Such a practice would vitiate the utility of arbitration as a quick and informal means for dispute resolution.

In addition to being succinct and definite, an arbitration award should also deal with all the issues raised by the parties and within the scope of the arbitrator's authority. 14 If the parties agree to arbitrate under the AAA patent rules, Rule 43 provides that the arbitrator has the authority to grant any remedy or relief deemed appropriate, including equitable remedies (i.e., specific performance of a contract or injunctive relief prohibiting infringement of a patent). 

B. Scope of the Award

An arbitrator may not exceed his authority in making the award for, in doing so, he provides one of the statutory grounds for vacation.15 The underlying agreement to arbitrate determines whether the arbitrator has exceeded his powers. 

As a general rule, if the arbitration award does no more than resolve the existing dispute between the parties to the arbitration, the arbitrator has not exceeded his powers. Thus, it is generally held that an arbitrator's award will not be set aside for errors in the application or interpretation of the law. 16 

Likewise, errors committed by the arbitrator in his findings of

14This does not mean that all issues must be addressed separately. An award which treats all issues collectively, either expressly or by implication, in the form of a definite sum of money is sufficient.

Edit t facts¹7 or in his interpretation of a contract¹8 are insufficient bases for vacating the arbitration award. However, an arbitration award which manifests a complete disregard of the applicable law19 or which demonstrates strong reliance upon an unambiguous and undisputed mistake of fact20 can be set aside under 35 U.S.C. §10(d) on the ground that the arbitrator has exceeded or abused his powers. 

Courts are and should be reluctant to set aside arbitration awards. The precept that an award may be vacated if it is based upon a manifest disregard of the law has been severely circumscribed because judicial review frustrates the utility of arbitration as a relatively quick and informal means for dispute resolution. Thus, it has been generally held that a "manifest disregard of the law" presupposes "something beyond and different from a mere error in the law or failure on the part of the arbitrators to understand or apply the law."21 The courts have required a showing that the arbitrator correctly understood and stated the law but proceeded to ignore it.22 Since arbitration awards typically contain no written opinion, this showing can almost never be made, and courts will not remand an award to an arbitrator in order to ascertain the reasons behind the award. Courts recognize that arbitration often compromises legal precision in favor of speed and informality. Before submitting a claim to arbitration it is therefore important to consider that reviewability is sacrificed. 

17 Orion Shipping & Trading Co. v. EasternStates Petroleum Corp. of Panama, S.A., 312 

F.2d 299, 300 (2d Cir.), cert. denied, 373 U.S. 949 (1963); Sidarma Societa Italiana di 

Armamento S.p.A. v. Holt Marine Indus., Inc., 515 F. Supp. 1302, 1308 (S.D.N.Y.), aff'd mem., 681 F.2d 802 (2d Cir. 1981). A distinction must be made between cases in which an arbitrator makes a mistake in a finding of fact and cases in which there is an evident material miscalcualtion of figures or an evident material mistake in the description of something referred to in the award. In the latter cases, a court may properly modify the award under 9 U.S.C. §11(a).

Ch. 8

Even in an instance where an arbitrator exceeds his powers, courts endeavor to save the award by severing the valid portions from the invalid portions if possible. 23 In those cases where the arbitrator enters an award upon a matter not submitted to him, 9 U.S.C. §11(b) specifically empowers a court to modify the award if the nonsubmitted matter does not affect the merits of the decision upon the matter actually submitted. Section 11 also contains a catchall subdivision (c), which allows a court to modify any award that is imperfect in a matter of form, not affecting the merits of the controversy. It should be noted that the language of §11 is not mandatory, and the courts may exercise discretion in applying it.24

As mentioned earlier, modifications of arbitration awards should not be directed towards the merits of the decision. An exception to this rule for patent arbitrations has been enacted in 35 U.S.C. §294(c). This section provides that the parties to a patent arbitration may agree that in the event a patent which is the subject matter of an award is subsequently adjudged to be invalid or unenforceable in a court, the award may be modified by a court upon application of either party. This type of modification would ordinarily, without the statutory exception, be impermissible since it goes to the merits of the decision.25 

C. Assessment of Costs, Fees, and Expenses 

The patent rules afford a very simple and equitable means for allocating the costs of an arbitration among the parties. 

Before examining these rules, however, it is worthwhile to consider as a comparison the rules and procedures which govern the taxation of costs in ordinary civil actions commenced in district court.

Because of the cooperative and informal nature of arbitration proceedings, even in the absence of a specific provision in the arbitration agreement dealing with costs, the parties usually agree to split many of the costs. 

In those instances, however, where the parties cannot come to terms, the patent rules afford a simple and equitable solution. 

Under the patent rules costs and expenses of the arbitration are allocated in one of three ways. First, certain expenses are charged exclusively to the party whose request gives rise to the expense. For example, although a stenographic record is not required under the rules, if one party requests such a record, Rule 23 provides that the cost is to be borne by the requesting party. The cost of any stenographic record of the witnesses' testimony, and all transcripts thereof, is prorated among all parties who order copies. Thus, the ordering of a transcript copy by a party who did not request a stenographic record in the first place obligates that party to pay for the transcript copy as well as his prorated share of the stenographic record. 

Rule 24 states that any party who desires an interpreter shall pay the costs of such service. It is further provided in Rule 

50 that the expenses of witnesses shall be paid by the party calling such witnesses. When documents are needed for judicial proceedings relating to the arbitration or for filing with the Patent and Trademark Office, the requesting party bears the expense. 33 The second way in which costs are allocated under the patent rules is the equal division among all parties to the arbitration. These types of costs include traveling expenditures of the arbitrator and the AAA representatives; the expenses of any witnesses or the cost of any proofs produced in direct response to a request of the arbitrator (in contrast to witnesses called by a party as provided for in Rule 50); and the arbitrator's fee.34

The third way in which arbitration costs and expenses can be allocated is at the discretion of the arbitrator in the award itself. Rule 50 provides that the arbitrator may assess any of the expenses described above, or any part thereof, against any specified party or parties. Rule 43 provides that the arbitrator

The AAA may require the parties to advance money towards the cost of the arbitration.36 In such instances, the AAA must render an accounting to the parties and return any unexpended balance. The AAA, as a nonprofit organization, has afforded parties to an arbitration, in the case of "extreme hardship," the opportunity to have the AAA administrative fee reduced or deferred.37

D. Attorneys' Fees 

***" The patent rules do not specifically provide for awards of attorneys' fees, and no court has ever addressed whether such awards are proper in a patent arbitration. It is generally held that attorneys' fees may not be granted by the arbitrator unless specified in the underlying agreement to arbitrate. Rule 43, however, states that the arbitrator "may grant any remedy or relief which the arbitrator deems just and equitable and within the scope of the agreement of the parties *** [and] shall assess arbitration fees and expenses in favor of any party Be- cause of the absence of a specific provision in the patent rules and the U.S. Arbitration Act43 dealing with attorneys' fees, arguably 35 U.S.C. §285 would apply. That section provides that reasonable attorneys' fees may be awarded to the prevailing party in a patent infringement action in "exceptional cases."'44

The contrary argument is that attorneys' fees are not awarded in U.S. courts unless a statute or agreement specifically provides so. 45

An arbitrator's decision with regard to attorneys' fees might be difficult to vacate or modify. As discussed previously,46 an arbitration award cannot be vacated only upon a showing of a mere error in the law or failure on the part of the arbitrator to understand or apply the law.47 Furthermore, since the applicable legal standard for either awarding or denying attorneys' 

43 Compare with N.Y. Civ. Prac. Law §7513 (McKinney 1980), which expressly prohibits the allowance of attorneys' fees in an arbitration award unless the parties agree otherwise. 

44 For a discussion of when a case is considered to be exceptional within the meaning of 35 U.S.C. §285, see 5 D. CHISUM, PATENTS §20.03 [4][c] (1986). 

45 The American rule with regard to attorneys' fees is that in the absence of an authorizing statute or contractual commitment, each party is responsible for its own attorneys' fees. Alyeska Pipeline Co. v. Wilderness Society, 421 U.S. 240, 10 FEP Cases 

826 (1975). 

46 See ch. 3, supra. 

47 See Fairchild & Co. v. Richmond F. & P. R.R. Co., 516 F. Supp. 1305 (D.D.C. 1981).

Ch. 95 

The Arbitrator's Award fees in patent cases is one of discretion, 48 an arbitrator's decision with regard to attorneys' fees arguably could not be vacated or modified based upon a disregard of the law. 

The issue of attorneys' fees arises frequently in judicial proceedings to confirm or vacate an arbitration award. In those situations, however, the attorneys' fees in dispute are generally those incurred in connection with the judicial proceeding itself, rather than the attorneys' fees incurred during the arbitration. 

The courts have not decided whether a court which entertains a motion to confirm, vacate, or modify an arbitration award can rely on 35 U.S.C. §285 as a basis for awarding attorneys' fees in connection with the motion.49 

Even assuming that §285 is inapplicable, federal courts still have "inherent [equitable] power" in a federal question case (as opposed to a case where jurisdiction is predicated on diversity of citizenship) to award attorneys' fees "where the losing party has acted in bad faith, vexatiously, wantonly or for oppressive reasons ***.50 Actions brought under 9 U.S.C. §§9, 10, or 11 to confirm, vacate, or modify an award made in a patent arbitration would be a federal question case, and in determining whether to award attorneys' fees in connection with federal question actions, courts have applied this bad faith test.51 Thus, it has been held that if a motion to vacate or modify 

48 See Hycor Corp. v. Schlueter Co., 740 F.2d 1529, 222 USPQ 553 (Fed. Cir. 1984); 

Penwalt Corp. v. Akzona, Inc., 740 F.2d 1573, 222 USPQ 833 (Fed. Cir. 1984). 

49 A motion to confirm, modify, or vacate a patent arbitration award is not a patent infringment action to which 35U.S.C. §285 is applicable. Generally, attorneys' fees may be awarded to the prevailing party under §285 in cases involving willful or deliberate infringement, fraud or inequitable conduct by the patentee in procuring the patent, or bad faith continuation of litigation by either party. Milgo Elec. Corp. v. United Business 

Communications, Inc., 623 F.2d, 645, 667, 206 USPQ 481, 498–499 (10th Cir.), cert. denied, 449 U.S. 1066, 208 USPQ 376 (1980); Kahn v. Dynamics Corp., 508 F.2d 939, 945, 184 

USPQ 260, 264 (2d Cir. 1974), cert. denied, 421 U.S. 930, 185 USPQ 505 (1975). Thus, awards of attorneys' fees under §285 necessarily involve an appraisal of the merits of a patent infringement claim or defense thereto. On the other hand, a motion to confirm, vacate, or modify an arbitration award does not involve an evaluation of the merits of the underlying dispute. Since the factors which are considered in awarding attorneys' fees under §285 are not even pertinent to a motion involving a patent arbitration award, it would seem that §285 should be inapplicable to such a motion.

Inc., 585 F. Supp. 1284, 1286 (N.D. III. 1984).

Ch. 8 

LIMU an arbitration award is brought "without justification" attorneys' fees incurred in connection with opposing the motion can be awarded. 52

In determining whether a party's refusal to abide by an arbitration award was "without justification" so as to justify an award of attorneys' fees to the prevailing party of the arbitration, it is not enough to conclude that the motion to vacate the award was unsuccessful. In Mobil Oil Corp. v. Independent Oil 

Workers Union,53 Mobil sought to vacate an arbitration award which reinstated a former employee. The district court granted the union's motion for summary judgment but denied its request for costs and attorneys' fees. Both parties appealed. 

In affirming the district court's decision refusing to award costs and attorneys' fees to the union, the Third Circuit held that although Mobil's challenge of the arbitration award failed,

Mobil did not demonstrate bad faith in bringing the motion to vacate. The court pointed to several factors which it considered in reaching its decision. First, the court noted that Mobil acted promptly in its attempt to vacate the arbitration award, evidencing its good faith belief in the legal sufficiency of its challenge. 54 Second, Mobil had not refused to comply with arbitration awards in the past. 55 Third, Mobil adduced "a substantial legal issue" which federal courts addressed in detail.56

It has been held that refusals to abide by arbitration awards based upon legally insufficient bases are "without justification" so as to permit recovery of attorneys' fees. In International Association of Machinists & Aerospace Workers v. Texas Steel Co.,57 the district court enforced a supplemental labor arbitration award against Texas Steel. The union moved for attorneys' fees incurred in obtaining confirmation of the award. The district court denied the union's motion because it found that although the company's profferred reasons for refusing to abide by the award were "meritless," they were not asserted "without justification."58 On appeal, the Fifth Circuit reversed the refusal to 

52 See cases cited in note 51, supra. 

53679 F.2d 299, 305, 110 LRRM 2620, 2624. 

54 Id. at 305, 110 LRRM at 2624. 

55 Id. This factor would seem to be more applicable to arbitrations of labor disputes than in any other situation because of the popularity of arbitration as the means for resolving such disputes. 

56 Id. 

57639 F.2d 279, 283, 106 LRRM 2985, 2988 (5th Cir. 1981). 

58 Id. at 279, 106 LRRM at 2986.

Ch. 97 

The Arbitrator's Award award attorneys' fees as an abuse of discretion. The appeals court found that Texas Steel's challenge of the award was based upon the merits of the dispute, which is never a legally sufficient basis.59

Thus, a party should make sure his challenge of an arbitration award is predicated upon one of the grounds set forth in 9 U.S.C. §§10 or 11.60 A party who fails to frame a challenge in such grounds runs the risk of having attorneys' fees assessed against him.

TIMUUL DRAW THUDAXNE CHAPTER 9 Enforcement of Awards 

Once the award is made, unless the losing party agrees to voluntarily pay the award, it must be enforced through some court proceeding. This chapter covers how awards may be enforced to obtain binding judgments through the courts. 

A. In the United States

District courts of the United States have the power to enter judgments upon arbitral awards.¹ These judgments may be enforced as though they were rendered in actions at law.² For example, they may be registered and enforced in other jurisdictions.3

The parties may agree that a particular district court may enter judgment. Failing that, the court of the district where the award was made may enter judgment. In either event, the following requirements must be met: (1) the parties must agree that judgment may be entered on the award;5 (2) the subject matter of the arbitration must be within federal subject matter jurisdiction, independently of Title 9;6 19 U.S.C. §9 (1970). 

29 U.S.C. §13; 28 U.S.C. §§1961 et seq. (1970). 

328 U.S.C. §1963 (1981). 

49 U.S.C. §9 

5Id. "General Atomic Co. v. United Nuclear Corp., 655 F.2d 968, 971 (S.D. Cal.), aff'd, 455 

F.2d 948 (9th Cir. 1981). 98

Ch. 99 

Enforcement of Awards 

(3) the court must have personal jurisdiction of the parties; and (4) the required documents must be filed with the court.8 

1. Agreement to Entry of Judgment on the Award 

The requirement that the parties agree to entry of judgment on the award is usually met, either explicitly or implicitly. The widely used American Arbitration Association (AAA) standard arbitration clause specifies that "judgment upon the award rendered by the Arbitrator(s) may be entered in any court having jurisdiction thereof." (See Appendix C.) In the absence of express agreement to entry of judgment, courts have had no difficulty finding implicit consent. Language in an agreement which provides that any decision of an arbitration panel "shall be final and binding upon both parties" has been deemed suf ficient to meet the requirements of 9 U.S.C. §9.9 Agreement to entry of judgment has also been found where the parties have specified that the disputes would be submitted to arbitration "in accordance with the rules of the American Arbitration Association."10 The AAA rules provide that "[p]arties to these 

Rules shall be deemed to have consented that judgment upon the arbitration award may be entered in any Court.' "/11 

2. Federal Subject Matter Jurisdiction 

Federal courts may enforce arbitral awards only where the subject matter is within the scope of federal jurisdiction. 12 Because the U.S. Arbitration Act does not itself grant subject matter jurisdiction,¹3 independent grounds of federal jurisdiction must be found on the basis of diversity and jurisdictional

This requirement should not be a barrier in arbitrations involving issues of patent validity, infringement, and enforceability.17 Section 294(a) of Title 35 specifically provides that arbitration of any "dispute relating to a patent validity or infringement shall be valid and enforceable."18 As to causes of action on patent licenses, if the arbitration involves only contract issues, an award might not be enforced in a federal court in the absence of diversity. Awards on contract issues that arise in the course of arbitration of patent validity and infringement issues should, however, be enforceable by a federal court under the doctrine of pendent jurisdiction.19

4. Documents for Entry of Judgment 

A petition for confirmation of an arbitral award and entry of judgment should state the basis of the court's jurisdiction over the parties and subject matter, and should describe the nature of the dispute and the arbitration. 2¹ In addition, 9 U.S.C. §13 specifies that the following documents must be submitted to the clerk with the motion for entry of judgment: "a) the agreement; the selection or appointment, if any, of an additional arbitrator or umpire; and each written extension of the time, if any, within which to make the award; "b) the award; "c) each notice, affidavit, or other paper used upon application to confirm, modify, or correct the award, and a copy of each order of the court upon such an application. The judgment shall be docketed as if it was rendered in action." 

If the arbitration relates to a dispute previously pending in the court where enforcement is sought, many of these documents will already be on file, but a new filing may be appropriate. 

B. In Foreign Countries 

Although in the vast majority of international arbitrations there is voluntary compliance with the arbitrator's decision,22 sometimes enforcement proceedings are necessary. There is no reported case of a court either refusing or granting enforcement of a patent arbitration award. 

21 See, e.g., 4 L. FRUMER, BENDER'S FEDERAL PRACTICE FORMS 405 (Form 4172) (1985). 

22 For instance, at least 90% of awards given under the International Chamber of 

Commerce (ICC) arbitration rules are spontaneously followed. de Nuzillet, Enforcement of Arbitral Awards, in ARBITRATION AND THE LICENSING PROCESS 3–3 (1984).

Ch. 9

1 2 

1. The New York Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Arbitral Awards 

The recognition and enforcement of arbitral awards in international commercial disputes have been facilitated by the wide acceptance of the 1958 United Nations Convention on the 

Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards ("New 

York Convention"). 23 This convention has been ratified or acceded to by at least 66 countries, including almost all major trading countries as well as many developing countries and most socialist countries. Under the convention, the national courts of the participating nations have tended to grant recognition and enforcement of foreign arbitral awards whenever possible.24 

Article I(3) allows a contracting state to place certain limitations on its obligations under the New York Convention. 

Some of these may have particular significance in patent disputes. One limitation, known as the "commercial reservation," gives a state the right to limit recognition and enforcement under the convention "only to differences arising out of legal relationships, whether contractual or not, which are considered as commercial under the law of the state making the reservation." Infringement is generally regarded as a tort, not a commercial matter.

A U.S. patentee seeking to enforce abroad an award of infringement may encounter an additional problem because the

United States has made both the commercial and the reciprocity (Article XIV) reservations. A foreign state that has not made the commercial reservation can therefore refuse enforcement by arguing that a foreign infringement award would not be enforceable in the United States and reciprocity is lacking. However, this problem has never arisen in reported nonpatent practice and so far has not precluded enforcement of an award.25 

Another potential difficulty lies in the variety of interpretations given to the word "commercial."26 For instance, an Indian court held that transfer of technology was not a commercial 

2321 U.S.T. 2517, T.I.A.S. No. 6997. 

24 Sanders, A Twenty Year's Review of the Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards, in ARBITRATION AND THE LICENSING PROCESS 3-14 (1984). 

25 A.J. VAN DEN BERG, THE NEW YORK ARBITRATION CONVENTION OF 1958: TOWARDS A UNIFORM JUDICIAL INTERPRETATION 14 (1981). 

26 Id. at 51.

Ch. 103 

Enforcement of Awards transaction.27 Similarly, Andean Pact Countries appear not to allow arbitration in the field of technology transfer.28 This may preclude enforcement of patent arbitration awards in these countries.

Enforcement may be more difficult in a country that is not a party to the New York Convention, or where the New York Convention is not applicable. Enforcement can be based on bilateral treaties or regional multilateral treaties, such as the Inter-American Convention on International Commercial Arbitration (Panama Convention),29 which many Latin American States have ratified. If no pertinent treaty exists, enforcement may be based on local law regarding the recognition of foreign judgments or arbitral awards. Most countries allow recognition of foreign arbitral awards or judgments on the basis of reciprocity, so long as the award meets the standards for enforcement contained in the New York Convention. It may be more effective to apply to the courts of the country where the award was made to confirm the award as a judgment, and then seek to enforce the judgment (as opposed to the award) in a second country. 

2. Procedural Aspects

The requirements imposed on a party seeking enforcement under the New York Convention are easy to fulfill. The party applying for judicial enforcement must supply the authenticated original award or a duly certified copy, as well as the original agreement to arbitrate or a duly certified copy.30 If the award or agreement is not in the language of the country where enforcement is sought, the applicant must also provide a certified translation of these documents. 

Individual nations may specify the form of the request for enforcement, and what authority shall be competent to hear the request. Other details of procedure, such as attachment, discovery, and set-off of a claim against the award, are also governed by local law. The convention limits contracting states 

27 Id. at 53. 

28 Id. at 54 n.128. 

29 Inter-American Convention on International Commercial Arbitration (1975). 

30 Since the convention does not specify what a "duly authenticated original or duly certified copy" is, this aspect must be determined by local law. For discussion see id. at 250-262. 10

Ch. 9

 from imposing "substantially more onerous conditions or higher fees or charges on the recognition and enforcement of arbitral awards to which this Convention applies than are imposed on the recognition or enforcement of domestic arbitral awards.”31 

There are three general approaches to regulating the procedure for enforcement of a convention award³2____ 

(1) enactment of specific provisions for enforcement of convention awards,33 (2) enactment of provisions for enforcement of all foreign arbitral awards34 or of all foreign arbitral awards and foreign judgments;35 and (3) enforcement pursuant to the same procedure as that for domestic awards.36 

3. Grounds for Refusing Enforcement of Awards 

A party's presentation of the documents enumerated in Article IV creates a presumption of an enforceable right against the other party, who may attempt to prove why the enforcing authority should refuse to acknowledge and enforce the award.

Grounds for refusing enforcement of an arbitral award, which are found in Article V of the New York Convention, fall into two basic categories: (1) the procedure is unfair to the parties involved; and (2) the substantive content of the decision violates public policy.

A court may refuse enforcement on its own motion if enforcing the award would violate international public policy of the forum (Article V(2)), but no review on the merits of the

Enforcement of Awards arbitral award is allowed in an enforcement proceeding.38 A party who wishes to challenge the decision of an arbitration must instead go to the courts of the country where the award was made and there request a setting aside or vacation of the arbitrator's decision. 

The enumerated grounds for refusal have been narrowly construed. Due process grounds have been accepted only in extreme cases of procedural irregularity.39 Public policy violations have been found only in cases where the international public order is disturbed.40

The enumerated grounds of Article V for refusing enforce ment are summarized below. The first two are particularly applicable to patent arbitrations, and are discussed in the next section. For a discussion of the others, see Albert Jan van den

(1) Enforcement of the award would be contrary to public policy 

(§2(b)). 

(2) The dispute cannot be subjected to arbitration according to the law of the country where enforcement is sought (§2(a)). (3) The parties were under some incapacity (§1(a)). (4) A party was not given notice of the proceedings (§1(b)). (5) The award is outside the submission to arbitration (§1(c)). (6) The arbitral tribunal was improperly gathered (§1(d)). (7) The arbitration agreement was invalid under the law the parties selected to govern the dispute or, if there is no choiceof-law clause, under the choice-of-law rules of the country where the award was made (§1(a)). Courts have construed this to mean that questions as to the form of the agreement are governed by Article II of the convention. 

(8) The award has not yet become binding or has been set aside in the country where it was made (§1(e)). 

4. Public Policy Objections 

Refusing enforcement of arbitral awards based on public policy grounds has the potential to act as a barrier to the rec- 

38 van den Berg, Commentary on Court Decisions on the New York Convention, IX YEARBOOK OF COMMERCIAL ARBITRATION 374 (1984). 

39 Id. at 373. 

40 Id. at 374. 

41 A.J. VAN DEN BERG, THE NEW YORK ARBITRATION CONVENTION OF 1958: TOWARDS A UNIFORM JUDICIAL INTERPRETATION 14 (1981). See also van den Berg, supra note 38 at 375.

Ch. 9

 ognition and enforcement of arbitration awards in patent disputes. The present trend among national courts, however, is to limit the public policy objection as much as possible. For example, in applying the convention, U.S. courts distinguish between domestic and international public policy, arguing that public policy in domestic relations does not necessarily pertain to public policy in international relations.42 This interpretation of Article V(2) was adopted by the U.S. Supreme Court in Scherk v. Alberto-Culver Co.,43 and was subsequently elaborated on by the Second Circuit in Parsons & Whittemore Overseas, Inc. v.

Societe General de L'Industrie Du Papier (RAKTA).44 The Second 

Circuit stated that "the Convention's public policy defense should be construed narrowly. Enforcement of foreign arbitral awards may be denied on this basis only where enforcement would violate the forum states' most basic notions of morality and justice."'45

Other national courts have also made this distinction. In particular, France, Italy, Switzerland, Germany, Mexico, Belgium, and Spain have all granted enforcement of arbitral awards despite objections by the respondent that the arbitration violated domestic public policy.46 Thus, "the public policy governing the enforcement of foreign arbitral awards is not domestic public policy, but the public policy of international law of the state where the decision is invoked."47 

5. New York Convention Countries Not Providing for Patent Arbitration Under Local Law 

Article V(2)(a) allows a court to refuse enforcement of an award on its own motion if the dispute could not be arbitrated under its own law. This should not, however, provide an absolute ban to enforcement. The distinction between domestic and international public policy, discussed above, limits the terms

Given the variety of approaches to arbitrability of patent matters in the contracting nations, patent arbitration can hardly be deemed to violate international public order. In cases where an arbitration award is granted in the country which issued the patent, one can argue that a country's public policy limitations regarding patents should be applicable only to that nation's patents, and the resulting award should be enforceable, regardless of how the enforcing nation limits arbitration of its own patents.

KAKVORITI AW VIDNARI CHAPTER 10 International Arbitration 

Various institutional arbitration mechanisms are available for resolution of international patent disputes. While a few countries freely permit patent arbitration, most restrict the use of arbitration to certain types of patent-related disputes. In the future it is likely that foreign countries will become more receptive to this form of dispute resolution, as has the United States.

The comments provided earlier in this book, including discussions of arbitrator selection and framing issues to be arbitrated, apply as well to international disputes. Indeed, arbitration is more likely to be utilized on an international scale where courts are apt to be suspected of national bias. The risk of bias is most prevalent when the subject matter of the dispute is closely tied to the national economy of one party's country. 

This national bias can be averted by choosing an arbitration forum in, and arbitrators from, a country other than that of either party.

While arbitration is generally espoused as saving time and money, at least one commentator believes that it is a more expensive alternative for international dispute resolution. He argues that the expense is greater due to administration translators and arbitrators' fees,¹ additional counsel, and transportation costs.2 The need for extra translators is apparent if the 

¹For a comparative guide to administrative and arbitrator's fees, see generally Bran son & Tupman, Selecting an Arbitral Forum: A Guide to Cost-Effective International Arbi tration, 24 VA. J. INT'L L. 917 (1984). 

'De Vries, International Commercial Arbitration: A Contractual Substitute for National 

Courts, 57 TUL. L. Rev. 42, 61 n.3 (1982). 108

Ch. 109 

International Arbitration situs and language of the proceeding is chosen such that it is foreign to both parties; instead of one party needing a translator, both will. The total costs for transportation may not be higher if the parties agree to a mutually accessible arbitration situs. When considering the costs of translators and transportation, a party should note that the proceeding is expedited so the overall time for which a translator is required or the number of trips to the proceeding from a home country may be less than in litigation. In sum, estimating costs is a difficult task and each party's circumstances must be evaluated.

A. International Tribunals 

This section points out some of the significant differences between several of the major international arbitration institutions and conventions in addition to the American Arbitration Association (AAA), discussed in earlier chapters. It is not intended to be a substitute for carefully reading the rules of an institution under consideration. 1. International Chamber of Commerce (ICC) Court of Arbitration 

The ICC Court of Arbitration is not a court but the name of the ICC institution which oversees the procedural aspects of 

3W. CRAIG, W.W. PARK, & J. PAULSSON, INTERNATIONAL CHAMBER of Commerce AR- BITRATION (1984). 

1

Ch. 10

 the arbitration. The ICC is located in Paris, where its administrative functions are accomplished. Its rules provide, however, that unless the parties specify otherwise, the arbitration actually takes place in a country other than that of either party." 

Before parties begin the arbitration process, the ICC rules provide access to a Conciliation Committee. The ICC rules do not express a preference for the number of arbitrators, which can, therefore, be set by the parties. The arbitrator determines procedural aspects such as the language of the proceeding. If the answering party challenges the validity of the agreement, the court makes a prima facie determination of its existence and the arbitrator then rules on its validity. The collection of physical evidence is hastened by rules requiring supporting documents to be filed with the request and answer.

A unique requirement of the ICC rules is the drafting of a Terms of Reference, which summarizes the parties' claims and defines the issues to be decided. A sincere effort is made to draft a Terms of Reference to which the parties will agree and sign. While the rules provide for continuing the proceeding if the parties cannot agree, this requirement does cause significant delays. 5 The ICC rules require the arbitrator to provide reasons for the award unless the parties explicitly state that they should not be given. 

2. Arbitration Institute of the Stockholm Chamber of Commerce 

(AISCC) The AISCC uses Swedish arbitration law to fill gaps not covered by its own rules. This institute favors three arbitrators when the parties have not specified otherwise. Regardless of the number chosen, the institute selects one arbitrator. Thus if only one arbitrator is used, he or she is not selected by the parties. The appointment of the arbitral tribunal starts the proceeding and an award is due within one year. The award must contain reasons supporting the decision. International Arbitration the AISCC has been a very popular forum for parties wishing to arbitrate.7 

3. Foreign Trade Arbitration Commission at the U.S.S.R. Chamber of Commerce and Industry The Foreign Trade Arbitration Commission will entertain an arbitration proceeding between parties who have agreed to arbitrate in writing or when the defendant's conduct evidences a voluntary submission to its jurisdiction. As this rule does not define such conduct, it is potentially troublesome to the unwary defendant. Final decisions on the commission's competence are made by the tribunal considering the case. The arbitrators are chosen by the parties but must come from a list of persons designated by the U.S.S.R. Chamber of Commerce. This list is required to contain only 15 names.

Hearings are conducted in Russian, though the commission has the authority to use a different language if the parties consent. The rules require that a record of the hearing be kept in each case by a reporter appointed by the commission. A statement of the reasons for the award is also required by the rules.

Court, when parties choose to use more than one arbitrator, the court appoints an arbitrator to act as chairman of the arbitral tribunal. The arbitrators determine the language of the proceeding and any questions as to their own jurisdiction. Unless the parties stipulate otherwise, the rules require that the award include reasons for the tribunal's decision. These rules¹0 are presented together since the IACAC adopted the substantive provisions of the UNCITRAL Arbitration Rules. The UNCITRAL rules were adopted by the United

The arbitral tribunal decides objections to its jurisdiction and is required to state the reasons for its award unless the parties agree otherwise. 

B. United Nations Convention on the Recognition and 

Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards 

Even a brief introduction to arbitration institutions and conventions would be incomplete if it did not mention the 

Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards. 11 The convention provides an enforcement mechanism that gives force to arbitral awards around the world. 

The U.S. Supreme Court underscored the importance of the public policy expressed in the convention in favor of liberalizing the recognition and enforcement of such awards: "The goal of the Convention, and the principal purpose underlying American adoption and implementation of it, was to encourage the recognition and enforcement of commercial arbitration agreements in international contracts and to unify the standards by which agreements to arbitrate are observed and arbitral awards are enforced in the signatory countries."'¹2

In sum, countries that sign the convention agree to enforce 

10 Rules of Procedure of the Inter-American Commercial Arbitration Commission 

(April 1982). 

¹¹Convention on the Recognition and Enforcement of Foreign Arbitral Awards of 

June 10, 1958. Pub. L. No. 91-368 (1970), which is ch. 2 of the U.S. Arbitration Act, 9 

U.S.C. §§201 et seq. The law provides that the district courts have jurisdiction to order arbitration and appoint arbitrators. Within three years, an arbitrator award may be confirmed. 

12 Scherk v. Alberto Culver Co., 417 U.S. 506, 520 n.15 (1974).

International Arbitration awards granted in other signatory countries. 13 The convention only applies where the arbitration relates to foreign matters or where one party is a foreigner. 

The convention contains limited exceptions under which a country may refuse enforcement. 14 These exceptions can be broken down into two general classes. First, enforcement may be refused where the award itself was defective because of improper notice to a party or if it was outside the scope of the arbitration clause. Second, where the subject matter of the dispute is not capable of settlement by arbitration or enforcement would be contrary to the public policy of the state where enforcement is sought, enforcement may be denied. 

C. Arbitrability of International Patent Disputes in Selected Countries 

The following is a review of the present state of patent arbitrability in selected countries. 15 

1. Japan

Patent arbitration is not practiced in Japan. 16 The National 

Report on Japan in the Yearbook Commercial Arbitration reports that matters which are not subject to compromise are not arbitrated. 17 As an example, the report cites the technical scope of a patent. While not arbitrable, the matter may be referred to the Director-General of the Patent Office for its determination. 18 

It is unlikely that parties would be able to settle disputes concerning the validity of a patent by compromise so these disputes are also excluded from arbitration.

 2. Federal Republic of Germany 

Patent arbitration is permitted in the Federal Republic of Germany, provided that both the patentee and the alleged infringer agree to submit the dispute to arbitration. No special conditions are placed upon the patent arbitration process, which is governed by the arbitration procedures set forth in the civil code. 

3. United Kingdom

Under the Arbitration Act of 1950, commercial disputes including questions of patent infringement and validity are subject to arbitration when the parties agree.19 The 1977 Patent Act provides for arbitration, if the parties agree, of disputes arising from the proposed grant of a compulsory license.20 

4. France

Under the French patent law of 1978 arbitration of patentdisputes is permissible provided that the arbitration would not require a partial or total invalidation of a patent. 21 Decisions as to patent validity are considered to affect the public order and are not arbitrable. 

5. Belgium

The law of Belgium is very accepting of patent arbitration. 

Patent validity, 22 infringement, and other disputes involving patents can be arbitrated as long as the parties have agreed.23 

While rights of third parties could conceivably be affected, patents are mainly viewed as a property right belonging to the

International Arbitration patentee.24 This view forms the basis of the broad freedom to arbitrate patent disputes in Belgium. 

The exceptions to the right to arbitrate are relatively few. 

Matters involving the loss of patent rights due to nonpayment of the annual tax or nonexploitation of the invention are solely within the jurisdiction of the administration and may not be submitted to an arbitration tribunal.25 If these claims arise during arbitration, the arbitrators are not qualified to hear them and need not consider them.26 Petitions for obligatory licenses form another category of patent disputes which must be submitted to the proper authorities and are therefore nonarbitrable.27 Thus the exceptions to the general trend of permitting arbitration fall in a fairly narrow class of patent-related disputes. 

6. Italy

Italian patent law states that all patent proceedings shall be brought before Italian courts, irrespective of the parties' nationality, domicile, or residence. This law has been narrowly construed and, in effect, only requires courtroom proceedings when one party claims that a patent is invalid.28

In Italy the validity of a patent is in a category of civil actions classified as being in the "public interest." The protection of this public interest is the responsibility of the public prosecutor. 

This duty is viewed as being so important that the prosecutor's presence in any public interest litigation is mandated by statute. 

The public prosecutor is also statutorily forbidden from participating in arbitration.29 Therefore, it would seem that all questions of patent validity must be settled in the courts. 

The rule is limited even further, however, since the public prosecutor's presence is only required when one party is applying to a court for a declaration that a patent is invalid.30 If invalidity is merely asserted as a defense, it does not have the

The Hong Kong patent system may eliminate one class of potential arbiters, i.e., patentees, but it does not reduce the number of potential defendants seeking to avoid litigation in patent-related disputes. Hong Kong manufacturers, whether licensees or alleged infringers, may be subject to suits by foreigners which could be resolved through arbitration.

CHAPTER 11 Other Alternative Dispute Resolution Procedures par- In formulating the best course of action to take for the client, the patent practitioner should always be mindful of other viable alternative dispute resolution methods. These methods include the more familiar mediation/conciliation, where the ties select an outside facilitator, usually with a specialized background; and the traditional form of negotiation, where no thirdparty mediator is involved but where the parties themselves hash out their differences. Each of these methods is nonbinding, as opposed to the usual arbitration proceeding, and leads to a mutually agreeable settlement. 

In recent years, a new form of dispute resolution has come into use. It is basically a hybrid of the existing methods, combining many of the features of mediation and negotiation, as well as arbitration and adjudication. It has been referred to in the past by many names, including mock trial, information exchange, advisory proceeding, and nonbinding arbitration, but has most popularly come to be known as the mini-trial. 

The mini-trial is really not a trial at all; it is an abbreviated presentation of the issues and facts in a dispute to the principals of the parties, that is, the high-level officers of each disputant, who then rationally review the situation and together formulate a satisfactory settlement. The actual presentation, which is in effect an exchange of information accumulated through discovery, is frequently presided over by a technical advisor selected by both parties. The advisor keeps the presentation 118

flowing, and if asked to, makes certain procedural rulings or provides an advisory opinion as to what the outcome would be had the dispute been adjudicated. 

This chapter provides a general description of the minitrial and its applicability to a patent dispute. For a more detailed description, the reader should consult the numerous texts and publications listed in the Bibliography, or any one of a number of dispute resolution centers, which can provide information on the more technical aspects of dispute resolution. 

A. Comparison of Various Forms of Dispute Resolution 

In order to appreciate some of the basic differences among the various forms of dispute resolution, this section compares in simplified terms the features of each, including arbitration (which is discussed at length previously in this book) and adjudication, as background. 

1. Adjudication 

Adjudication is a nonvoluntary dispute resolution avenue. 

In many situations, where pure legal issues or questions of witness credibility provide the fulcrum for the dispute, this may be the best and, perhaps, only course of action to pursue. 

In a judicial resolution the presentation of the case is directed to a third-party neutral decision-maker (or group of decision-makers). The parties to the dispute have very little control, if any, over who that decision-maker is; the decision-maker, therefore, may be a person with little or no technical expertise in the subject matter of the litigation. 

Adjudication is a highly procedural and structurally rigid form of dispute resolution, governed by an inflexible set of rules. Each party is given an opportunity to present its case in a formal setting, with the final outcome being a binding decision where one party wins and the other party loses. 

Adjudication, though relatively expensive, does result in fairly consistent resolutions, which allows the parties, prior to the decision, to predict the outcome with some accuracy. Of course, a litigated dispute is a public proceeding, and the parties, therefore, have little control over keeping any portion of the dispute private.

Ch. 11

2. Arbitration 

Unlike court adjudication, arbitration is a voluntary attempt by the parties to resolve the dispute, although it may have been, initially, contractually imposed. It usually results in a binding resolution and may be a compromise of the parties' positions rather than the win/lose result of adjudication. A thirdparty decision-maker is selected by the parties, which gives them leeway to select a decision-maker with special expertise in the area of dispute.

Procedurally, arbitration is less formal than adjudication; the rules governing the procedural and substantive aspects of the arbitration hearing can be selected by the parties. Each party is given an opportunity to present its case to the decision-maker in the most favorable light. A determination of the issues is probably less consistent and predictable than in adjudication where the relationship of the parties does not influence the final determination.

An arbitration is usually a private resolution, although its results may become public if court enforcement is sought. 

3. Mediation/Conciliation 

Mediation or conciliation is a voluntary attempt by the parties to settle the dispute without court adjudication and without resorting to a binding arbitration. This form of dispute resolution leaves the parties in full control of the outcome. It is a nonbinding process where the emphasis is on the parties themselves resolving their differences. 

A mediator selected by the parties and usually possessing technical expertise in the subject matter of the dispute acts as the catalyst. Each side's case is presented to the mediator in an informal and procedurally unstructured manner. The attitudes and relationship of the parties have a strong influence on the settlement that is reached, which is primarily a compromise of each side's position.

Mediations or conciliations of disputes are private and usually contractual, although many situations arise where the parties agree to use this method after the battle lines have been drawn.

4. Traditional Negotiation 

The least structured of the alternative methods to adjudication is the traditional negotiation process. It results in a resolution of the dispute only if cool heads and rational thinking prevail. Therefore, the relationship and attitudes of the parties play a strong role in the negotiation. There is no third-party decision-maker or mediator; the parties are left to themselves to formulate a satisfactory settlement, usually in the form of a compromise.

There is usually no presentation of each side's proof during negotiation, although each side may advance a principled argument. Negotiations have no particular form; that is, there are no rules of procedure that the parties must adhere to. It is, of course, a method of dispute resolution which is entirely private to the disputants. 

5. The Mini-Trial 

As stated previously, the mini-trial is a method that combines many of the features of other dispute resolution processes. For example, as in adjudication, the mini-trial provides for the presentation of proofs and arguments; however, it usually results in a mutually agreeable settlement, rather than the win/lose result of adjudication (and often arbitration). 

A third-party "neutral advisor" is chosen by the parties to officiate the proceedings. The advisor may enjoy a technical expertise in the area. The advisor's function is to keep the procedure flowing naturally and, if requested, to provide an advisory opinion as to how the dispute would have been resolved had it been adjudicated. This opinion, in conjunction with the parties' knowledge of the strong and weak points of their positions developed through the proof presentation, often spurs the parties on to a favorable settlement. Of course, the neutral advisor's opinion is merely advisory and has no binding effect.

The parties are free to choose how they would like to proceed. The presentation of evidence and arguments, or the "information exchange," is a relatively short process before the neutral advisor and officials for each party who have settling authority. On the basis of the presentation and, if desired, the

Ch. 11

 neutral advisor's opinions, the officers for the disputants discuss the relevant issues and strive for an agreeable business solution with little or no consideration of procedural legal aspects.

B. Procedure of Mini-Trial 

The mini-trial, by its very nature, is loosely structured; the parties have full control over how the mini-trial takes form and over all contingencies (for example, whether the mini-trial should to be nondispositive of the dispute). It must be initially decided if the dispute is one to which the mini-trial is most applicable. 

The mini-trial can work in a variety of disputes. However, in every case, the mental state of each disputant is a determinative factor in the success or failure of the mini-trial. Even if communications between the parties has broken down to a point where traditional negotiation is impossible, the parties may still retain sufficient rationality to desire to resolve the dispute as favorably as possible with the least cost and delay, and the lowest risk. It is, therefore, the motivation of the disputants which makes the case ripe for the mini-trial. The disputants should be sufficiently flexible to negotiate the procedure they wish to follow in conducting the mini-trial. 

Experience shows that the mini-trial works well in cases involving mixed questions of law and fact, such as in patent cases. Where the main issue in a case involves the credibility of witnesses or purely legal questions, adjudication or arbitration may be the better way to proceed. If technical factual issues are involved, which would require protracted expert testimony in litigation, there is no reason why such a case cannot be resolved by a mini-trial with a technically competent neutral advisor.

The stage to which the dispute has progressed is also a determining factor in whether a mini-trial is an appropriate alternative to adjudication and other dispute resolution forms. 

If there has been some discovery in the case, the disputants may be sufficiently apprised of the issues, and the strengths and weaknesses of their positions, to be able to assess accurately the risks and costs of protracted litigation and whether other alternatives to litigation are viable. 

If it is determined that the mini-trial may be a possible way

to proceed, counsel (or management) should contact the other party and "loosely" suggest a mini-trial. If the other party is receptive to the mini-trial idea, the next step is to work out the structure of the mini-trial, that is, how it is to proceed, including a timetable of events. The procedural issues of the mini-trial should be handled first, and, after the ice is broken, the more "explosive" substantive issues can be discussed. The actual procedural details of the mini-trial should be a joint effort between the parties; this not only encourages the likelihood that the mini-trial will bear a favorable outcome, but also ensures that the process meets both parties' objectives.

There is no one form to follow in a mini-trial, which is an advantage in that the parties may tailor the process to fit their needs. However, there are some guidelines for preparing the schedule of events and rules of procedure and addressing some collateral and contingent issues of the case.

If litigation is pending, and depending upon what stage the litigation is in, the parties should decide whether to request a stay of the proceedings to conduct the mini-trial. How, when, and what rules apply to pre-mini-trial discovery should be discussed. Also, the issues to be addressed at the mini-trial may be framed, at least broadly, at this time. The parties should agree as to what their obligations are in presenting their arguments and negotiating after the presentation.

It should be decided who will attend the mini-trial, that is, whether experts or other witnesses will be present, and which principals of each disputant will hear the presentation. It is important that the principals who do attend possess the authority to settle, as they will be the primary negotiators following the mini-trial presentation. Also, when and where the minitrial and the subsequent negotiations will take place, and the order of presentation, should be agreed on at this time.

The parties should decide on what rules of evidence will apply and what documents (e.g., briefs) will be exchanged. The confidentiality of the proceeding and the otherwise inadmissible nature of the evidence exchanged and testimony given are important aspects of the mini-trial because they create an atmosphere of free and open exchange of thoughts so important in any settlement negotiation. These should be discussed, as well as sanctions for violations. Of course, it should also be decided by the parties how the costs of the mini-trial are to be apportioned.

Ch. 11

In these discussions, it should also be decided by the parties whether a neutral advisor will participate, how the selection will be made, and what the advisor's functions will be. It is easier for the parties to select a neutral advisor in this form of alternative dispute resolution than in an arbitration proceeding because the outcome of the mini-trial is not binding on either party. For this reason, the parties tend to select a neutral advisor who is competent in the technical field in dispute as well as credible to each, rather than selecting an advisor who will give either a procedural advantage in the mini-trial. 

The parties usually exchange names, propose advisors, and select those who are mutually agreeable. If the parties to the dispute need a list of advisors who are willing to serve, they can contact the Center for Public Resources (see below) or one of the many other dispute resolution organizations. The neutral advisors listed with these organizations are for the most part former judges, high-level government officials, and lawyers or businessmen who are specialized and competent in both legal and technical aspects of the subject matter in dispute. After a mutually agreeable advisor has been selected, both parties should contact the individual.

The next stage in the mini-trial procedure is discovery. By agreement, the discovery should be expedited and limited so as not to prolong the mini-trial. It may be agreed that the limited discovery for the mini-trial will not prejudice any party's right to obtain full discovery in the event settlement of the dispute after the mini-trial is not reached. If questions arise during premini-trial discovery, they can be submitted to the neutral advisor for an opinion.

In preparation for the actual information exchange during the mini-trial, the principals should carefully study the relevant issues and facts in the case so that they will get the full benefit of the information exchange and act knowledgeably during the post-mini-trial negotiations.

The actual mini-trial or information exchange usually lasts two or three days. Counsel for each party, the principals, any witnesses agreed upon by the parties, and the neutral advisor should be present. The sequence of each party's presentation, as well as time limitations for each party, are agreed upon in advance. This forces counsel to focus on the major issues in the case, and keeps the mini-trial limited to just that—a minitrial.

Typically, the plaintiff first presents its case, followed by the defendant's rebuttal. The plaintiff then responds to the defendant's rebuttal. This is followed by an open question and answer exchange. The defendant then presents its case, which, in turn, is followed by the plaintiff's rebuttal. The defendant then responds to the rebuttal. A second open question and answer exchange occurs at this time. The format is analogous to a debate, with sufficient time given to each side to present its case. It should be remembered that the presentation is being made to the principals attending the mini-trial, and counsel's presentation, therefore, should be tailored to match the principal's knowledge and expertise in the subject matter under dispute.

Post-mini-trial negotiations follow immediately after the information exchange. The principals should meet without the neutral advisor and negotiate a settlement. If no settlement is reached, the neutral advisor may be requested to submit an opinion as to the merits of each party's case. After the advisor's opinion has been given, a second negotiation takes place between the principals, perhaps with the neutral advisor present (although in most cases the advisor is not present).

C. Illustrative Patent Mini-Trials 

The first widely publicized mini-trial, which coincidentally happened to be a patent infringement case, was conducted in 1977 and involved the companies TRW and Telecredit, Inc.¹ At issue was the infringement of a number of patents relating to computerized check verification, charge authorization, and automated cash-dispensing systems. Telecredit claimed $6,000,000 in damages for past infringement and sought an injunction against future infringement.

The case had been in federal court for three years and had consumed several hundred thousand dollars in legal fees on both sides. Traditional negotiations between the parties were unproductive and the parties did not seek arbitration for several reasons, including their lack of control over the final disposition of the dispute.

One of the concerns of the parties also was the lack of 

¹Telecredit, Inc. v. TRW, Inc. and TRW Data Sys. Inc., C.D. Cal. No. CV 74–1172-RF (1977).

Ch. 11

Dou ווד In enforceability of arbitration in patent cases. Section 294 of Title 

352 had not even been proposed in 1977, and case law at that time seemed to indicate that patent infringement was not an arbitrable dispute. For these reasons, TRW and Telecredit turned to the mini-trial concept to resolve the dispute. 

The procedure worked out by the parties consisted basically of a nonbinding information exchange before high-level corporate officials and a privately hired neutral advisor. The procedure took several months to organize and only two days for the information exchange or the actual mini-trial. Within a half hour of completion of the presentations, the parties negotiated a mutually agreeable settlement. 

The neutral advisor in the proceeding moderated the minitrial but did not try to effect a settlement. As agreed by the parties, the neutral advisor was to submit a nonbinding, written opinion as to the strengths and weaknesses of each side's position, in the event the two sides could not reach an understanding.

Only the most essential pretrial discovery was conducted during the six weeks prior to the information exchange. During the mini-trial itself, formal rules of evidence were suspended, and the parties were permitted to decide for themselves what evidence and arguments would be presented. The parties agreed that any evidence or statements presented during the mini-trial would be inadmissible in court should no settlement be reached, and that the neutral advisor could not be a witness at a later trial.

Although the actual costs for the mini-trial incurred by the parties were not revealed, it was estimated that the costs to litigate the dispute would have been 10 times greater. In the 

TRW-Telecredit dispute, the mini-trial procedure shortened what appeared to be a costly and protracted litigation. 

More recently, the mini-trial has been even more effectively used to head off a threatened patent infringement suit. In 1979 the owner of a patent for some highly sophisticated electronic technology and the franchisee of a prospective infringer conducted a mini-trial to resolve a patent infringement dispute. 

No litigation had been filed, but the franchisee was worried 235 U.S.C. §294 (1984). This section renders agreements to arbitrate issues of patent validity and infringement enforceable. See generally ch. 2, infra.

about its exposure for prospective infringement of the owner's patents, although the franchisor believed there was no infringement.

The patent owner and the franchisee selected a neutral advisor who was a senior partner in a large patent law firm. 

The parties presented their cases at the information exchange, and the neutral advisor met individually with both sides and personally tested the product that was the subject of the patents until he was convinced that he understood both side's positions.

After concluding his investigation, the neutral advisor issued a nonbinding written opinion that infringement of the patents would likely occur. The franchisee presented the advisor's opinion to the franchisor who, after studying the advisor's opinion and although he was not a party to the minitrial, settled with the patent owner by taking a license on the patents in issue.

Another interesting mini-trial was conducted in 1979 between the Shell Oil Company and Intel Corporation after suit for patent infringement was filed but prior to discovery. What is interesting about this mini-trial is that it used an arbitrational format in that the decision of the advisor was binding on the parties. 

Shell owned 30 patents on high-speed random access memories and, after apprising Intel of its claim, filed a suit for patent infringement. Emotional issues were involved, but in spite of this the parties were able to narrow the issue to one Shell patent and one semiconductor memory manufactured by Intel. Intel agreed not to contest the validity of the patent. The issue of infringement was submitted to a reputable neutral patent attorney who acted as the advisor. The parties also drafted a license agreement in advance, covering the Shell patents and the semiconductor devices manufactured by Intel. A blank space was left in the agreement for the advisor to insert his decision indicating whether additional royalty payments would be due. 

The parties decided that live testimony would be unnecessary at the mini-trial, and an expedited discovery schedule was set up which was similar to that used in the TRW-Telecredit dispute.

The actual mini-trial took one and one-half hours for each party. The witness testimony was in the form of deposition

Ch. 11

1 transcripts and, based on the oral and written presentations of the parties, the advisor, resolving the dispute, rendered a decision within 30 days after the mini-trial.

Another mini-trial involved the theft of trade secrets and patent infringement. The Gillette Company, which owned several patents on writing instruments, brought suit for patent infringement and theft of trade secrets against two former executives who had left the employ of Gillette to join a competitor. 

The competitor began marketing the same writing instrument manufactured and marketed by Gillette.

Even though suit had been filed, the parties decided to proceed in a mini-trial without a neutral advisor. A schedule was set up, calling for limited document discovery and examination of parties. The entire proceeding took six months, with the defendant taking a license on the Gillette patents and Gillette taking an option on any patents that the competitor might obtain.

Mini-trials have been used to resolve a variety of disputes, including a products liability case;³ an employee-discharge dispute, where the employer wanted to avoid embarrassing publicity involving a senior executive, and the employee wanted to protect his reputation and keep his lucrative position; a securities and land fraud case; an antitrust dispute, which in effect was a court-centered mini-trial; and a multiparty corporationgovernment contract dispute.5 The mini-trial has also been effectively used by employees to resolve their grievances against management short of arbitration."

D. Private and Public Dispute Resolution Services 

There are a number of private and public dispute resolution services available for consultation. Some services provide the forum, propose third-party neutral advisors, and suggest rules of procedure and evidence for the mini-trial and other forms of dispute resolution. These services include profit and non- 

3 Automatic Radio Inc. v. TRW, Inc., 1980. *Heublin v. Clorox, N.D. Cal. 1973.

National Aeronautics and Space Admin. v. Space Communications Co. and TRW, Inc. 

"United Mine Workers of Am. v. Bituminous Coal Operators Ass'n.

profit corporations and bar associations. Some of these services are discussed briefly below. 

1. The Center for Public Resources 

The Center for Public Resources' is a private, nonprofit organization which is active in studying and promoting minitrials and other forms of alternative dispute resolution techniques. The center is sponsored and financed by many large corporations, law firms, and foundations. Its legal program provides a host of services, including dispute management educational programs, which provide speakers, seminars, and workshops to corporate legal departments, law firms, bar associations, and judicial conferences. The programs cover such topics as alternative dispute resolution, litigation management, dispute prevention, and judicial application of alternative dispute resolution techniques.

To promote more information on alternatives to litigation, the legal program of the Center for Public Resources sponsors academic research projects and experimentation. Thus, the center's legal program helps to identify, analyze, and communicate practices developed by corporations, law firms, and others involving alternative dispute resolution.

The center also provides a number of publications to assist in providing the latest information in resolving and managing disputes. These include a monthly newsletter Alternatives; Corporate Dispute Management, published by Matthew Bender, which is a handbook of practices that resolve, prevent, or manage disputes cost effectively; educational video tapes, including one on the mini-trial; and a computer-based clearinghouse that provides comprehensive information on alternative dispute resolution cases and developments.

The Center for Public Resources also provides a judicial panel whose members act as dispute resolution counselors and neutral advisors. The panel members can also be called on to help the parties design a fair and cost-effective private dispute resolution procedure, and manage discovery and discoveryrelated disputes.

2. Endispute

EndisputeⓇ is a for-profit business that provides a variety of dispute resolution services. As one example of its scope, its panel was used to help organize a resolution procedure to settle property-damage insurance claims caused by hurricane Iwa. 

Endispute proposes several different approaches, including a nonbinding settlement conference, a mini-arbitration, and a mini-trial tailored to fit the needs of each dispute. 

3. The National Institute for Dispute Resolution (NIDR) 

The National Institute for Dispute Resolution is a researchoriented foundation that provides grants to fund research projects involving alternative dispute resolution techniques. 

4. Federal Bar Council

The Federal Bar Council10 has named a number of trial lawyers to a panel to settle or decide disputes involving members' clients and to arbitrate partnership or fee matters. 

5. Judicial Arbitration Service 

The Judicial Arbitration Service is a California-based business offering a number of different public and private dispute resolution processes. Through the judicial Arbitration Service, parties can choose a neutral third party and arbitration procedures, binding or voluntary settlement conferences, or structured settlement procedures. 

6. Bar Associations 

The Denver Bar Association has developed a voluntary system of resolving disputes outside the state court system. If

The panel members chosen to hear the dispute are selected from a list of Denver attorneys. A maximum of three attorneys may be selected to hear the dispute, but it is recommended that only one referee be used. Selected reading materials may be provided to the referees by the parties up to three days before the hearing. After the hearing, the referee orally recommends a settlement and provides a nonbinding, written evaluation of the dispute. 

7. Other Dispute Resolution Services 

There exist a number of other alternative dispute resolution services available for consultation. Judicate¹¹ is a Philadelphiabased company which is in effect a private court system. This company was founded in 1983, went public in 1985, and now covers 41 states. Judicate contracts with more than 240 former judges who apply the appropriate laws and follow procedures similar to those applicable in the federal courts.

Several universities have initiated dispute resolution centers. These include Duke University and the University of Missouri.

Private industry has also developed services that can handle specialized disputes. For example, the National Advertising 

Division (NAD), in conjunction with the National Advertising 

Review Board (NARB), handles complaints in the advertising industry. The NAD makes initial decisions on complaints prior to review by the NARB. The NARB reviews written evidence and oral testimony from the NAD, the advertisers and witnesses, and then issues written judgments, to which advertisers have ten days to respond. If the advertiser refuses to comply, the NARB reports its findings to a government agency. 

Some corporations employ a complaint officer to prevent and to resolve employee disputes. The officer is a neutral advisor who first tries to apply the usual grievance procedure to resolve the dispute. If the dispute cannot be resolved by con-
